## 1. Background information on the procedure

## 1.1. Type II variation

Pursuant to Article 16 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 1234/2008, Roche Registration GmbH submitted to the European Medicines Agency on 30 October 2025 an application for a variation.

The following variation was requested:

| Variation(s) requested   | Variation(s) requested                                                              | Type              |
|--------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|-------------------|
| C.I.6.a                  | C.I.6.a Addition of a new therapeutic indication or modification of an approved one | Variation type II |

Extension of indication to include treatment of paediatric patients aged 10 years and older with relapsing remitting multiple sclerosis (RRMS) for OCREVUS, based on primary analysis results from the pivotal phase III study (WN42086/OPERETTA 2) and primary and updated results from a supportive phase II study (WA39085/OPERETTA 1). OPERETTA 1 is an open-label, parallel-group, dose-finding Phase II study to determine the dosing regimen of ocrelizumab to be further investigated in OPERETTA 2, and OPERETTA 2 is a Phase III, randomized, double-blind, double-dummy, parallel-group, multicenter, non-inferiority study to evaluate the efficacy and safety of intravenous ocrelizumab in comparison with fingolimod. As a consequence, sections 2, 4.1, 4.2, 4.4, 4.5, 4.6, 4.8, 5.1, 5.2 and 6.6 of the SmPC are updated. The Package Leaflet is updated in accordance. Version 15.0 of the RMP has also been submitted. In addition, the Marketing authorisation holder (MAH) took the opportunity to introduce updates to other sections of the SmPC and PL as per previous procedures linguistic review comments (sodium, pH and osmolality), updates to comply with the Excipient Guideline (polysorbates), changes to the list of local representatives in the Package Leaflet, as well as editorial and clarification changes to the product information (PI).

The variation requested amendments to the Summary of Product Characteristics, Package Leaflet, and to the Risk Management Plan (RMP).

## Information on paediatric requirements

At the time of submission of the application, the PIP EMA/PE/0000265579 had been completed.

The PDCO issued on 14 November 2025 a positive opinion on compliance for PIP EMA/PE/0000265579.

## 2. Scientific discussion

## 2.1. Introduction

## 2.1.1. Problem statement

## Disease

Multiple sclerosis (MS) is a chronic immune-mediated inflammatory disorder, resulting in demyelination and neurodegeneration within the central nervous system. The clinical course of MS has historically been divided into relapsing remitting and progressive phenotypes. Although these forms are increasingly considered a spectrum rather than separate forms, the relapsing-remitting phenotype is the far most dominant in the paediatric population and progressive presentations of MS are extremely rare (Kappos et al., JAMA Neurology, 2020, Montalban et al., 2025 Lancet Neurology).

Published guidelines and consensus statements agree that a single diagnostic framework should be applied to adult onset MS (AOMS) and paediatric onset MS (POMS i.e. MS diagnosed before 18 years old), although the risk of overlooking another differential diagnosis is considered higher in POMS due to the lower a priori risk of MS in this population and the fact that MS diagnosis is based on exclusion of other more likely explanations (Montalban et al., 2025). In particular, patients &lt; 12 years of age require careful investigations and considerations on differential diagnosis (particularly acute disseminated encephalomyelitis (ADEM)). However, having taken this into consideration it is agreed that RMS in adults and children share similar pathobiological mechanisms and magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) features (Montalban et al., 2025)

Paediatric patients cannot per se be considered to have a milder disease, although they may recover more quickly from relapses, because it is well recognized that the paediatric population often experience high disease activity, higher relapse rates, and may reach disability milestones at a younger age than adults due to earlier onset (Montalban et al., 2025).

## State the claimed the therapeutic indication

Ocrevus is indicated for the treatment of paediatric patients aged 10 years and older with relapsing remitting multiple sclerosis (RRMS).

## Epidemiology

It is estimated that about 2-5% of RMS patients are diagnosed as children and/or adolescents, although this depends on the cohort in question. The incidence of paediatric MS is reported to be approximately 0.5-1:100.000 individuals in adolescents, in children aged &lt; 12 years old the incidence is lower.

## Clinical presentation, and prognosis

Most children with MS present with focal or multifocal neurologic syndromes similar to adults, but children with POMS have higher and more severe relapse rates, greater lesion burden, and increased brainstem and cerebellar involvement (Fay et al. 2012). Residual physical disability after the initial demyelinating event is present in less than 10% of children, compared to up to 25% of adults (Banwell 2014). Comparative magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) studies have shown a higher T2 lesion burden in children with MS compared with adults, supporting the concept that MS in children is more inflammatory than in adults (Waubant et al. 2009; Yeh et al. 2009). Although children with POMS tend to have good motor recovery after attacks, they reach disability at a younger age because of earlier disease onset, and a significant percentage of them report cognitive difficulties and declining school performance (Fay et al. 2012; Amato et al. 2014). As with adults, MS in the pediatric population is associated with fatigue and impairment in quality of life.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Management

In the EU teriflunomide is indicated for paediatric RMS without restriction in the age group 10-17 years. Dimethyl fumarate has a paediatric indication for RMS in children aged 13 years or older. Fingolimod is indicated for treatment of paediatric RMS in children aged 10-17 years old with highly active and/or treatment refractory RMS. Considering the highly active disease experienced by paediatric patients, and the well documented risk of disability later in life, there is a clear unmet need for highly-effective disease modifying treatments (HE-DMTs) that can be used in the paediatric population. Fingolimod can be considered a HE-DMT although it has a different safety profile and mechanism of action, including risk of auriculoventricular block and cardiological adverse effects, macular oedema, which occurs in 0.5% of patients, and severe disease rebound after treatment discontinuation.

## 2.1.2. About the product

Invented name(s): Ocrevus

Authorised pharmaceutical form(s): Concentrate for solution for infusion, solution for injection

Authorised route(s) of administration: Intravenous use (IV), subcutaneous use (SC)

## Currently, Ocrevus (intravenous (IV, SC administration) is indicated for

- the treatment of adult patients with relapsing forms of multiple sclerosis (RMS) with active disease defined by clinical or imaging features.
- the treatment of adult patients with early primary progressive multiple sclerosis (PPMS) in terms of disease duration and level of disability, and with imaging features characteristic of inflammatory activity.

The precise mechanisms through which ocrelizumab exerts its therapeutic clinical effects in MS is not fully elucidated but is presumed to involve immunomodulation through the reduction in the number and function of CD20-expressing B cells.

This Type II variation application seeks approval for a new indication for the approved medicinal product Ocrevus (ocrelizumab) 300 mg concentrate for solution for infusion (intravenous use) for paediatric patients aged 10 years and older with relapsing-remitting multiple sclerosis (RRMS).

## 2.2. Non-clinical aspects

No new clinical data have been submitted in this application, which was considered acceptable by the CHMP.

## 2.2.1. Ecotoxicity/environmental risk assessment

Ocrelizumab by its nature as a protein (monoclonal antibody (mAb)) is not posing any risk for the environment. The target of the mAb is not expected in living species in water, sediment or sewage.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Monoclonal antibodies are considered naturally occurring products (i.e. they are proteins), which are not expected to remain either stable or biologically active in the environment for any significant period of time because of their susceptibility to chemical and physical degradation, as well as biodegradation by a wide range of microflora, and various physical removal mechanisms. Hence, the MAH provided an adequate justification for not conducting environmental risk assessment studies.

## 2.2.2. Conclusion on the non-clinical aspects

Considering the above data, ocrelizumab is not expected to pose a risk to the environment.

The Clinical trials were performed in accordance with GCP as claimed by the MAH.

The MAH has provided a statement to the effect that clinical trials conducted outside the community were carried out in accordance with the ethical standards of Directive 2001/20/EC.

## 2.3.2. Bioanalytical methods

The pharmacokinetics (PK) and antidrug antibodies (ADA) assays for ocrelizumab were developed and validated under the direction of Genentech. This work was performed in accordance with current industry best practices and the performance parameters of these assays were within industry and health authority recommendations (ICH M10 2022).

## 2.3.3. Pharmacokinetics

No specific development of the drug product was conducted for the pediatric indication. The manufacturing process and controls for the product to be supplied to pediatric patients remain the same as for the commercially approved 300 mg/vial IV product.

## 2.3.3.1. PK data analysis

The PPK analysis was conducted via nonlinear mixed-effects modelling with the NONMEM software, Version 7.5.1 (ICON Development Solutions). Graphical and all other statistical analyses, including evaluation of NONMEM outputs, were performed using R 4.4.2 for Windows. The first-order conditional estimation with INTERACTION option method in NONMEM was employed for all model runs. Modelbased simulations were performed by a combination of R and NONMEM software.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.3.3.2. Population PK (PopPK) modelling

In OPERETTA 1, an IV dose of 300 mg ocrelizumab for patients with a body weight (BW) from ≥25 kg to &lt;40 kg was evaluated. An IV dose of 600 mg ocrelizumab for patients with a BW ≥40 kg was evaluated. In OPERETTA 2, for paediatric patients 10 years of age and older weighing &lt;35 kg, the recommended dosing regimen of ocrelizumab is 300 mg IV infusion and 600 mg for paediatric patients 10 years of age and older weighing≥35 kg.

As used for adult patients, the first dose of ocrelizumab was administered as 2 IV infusions of half the dose given 14 days apart (i.e., 2 x 150 mg for a total dose of 300 mg, and 2 x 300 mg for a total dose of 600 mg). Subsequent doses of ocrelizumab (i.e., 300 mg or 600 mg) were administered as single infusions.

PK samples were drawn on Days 1, 15, 29, 57, 85, 113, 169, 337, 505, and 673 (before administration of premedication prior to the ocrelizumab IV infusion). On infusion visits, two serum samples were collected, one 5-30 minutes prior to the methylprednisolone administration and the second one 30 (± 10) minutes following the completion of the infusion of ocrelizumab.

The analysis dataset contained 1164 quantifiable serum samples from 116 patients. Of these, OPERETTA 2 provided 847 concentrations from 93 patients and OPERETTA 1 contributed 317 concentrations from 23 patients. Of 1164 quantifiable observations, 4 post-dose quantifiable observations-outliers were excluded from the analysis. Below the limit of quantification concentrations (a total of 47 samples, or 3.9% of observations) were also not included in the analysis.

Mean (range) of body weight and age of the population were 66.5 kg (27 - 154 kg) and 14.9 years (10 - 17 years), respectively. OPERETTA 2 included only 1 subject aged 11 years and OPERETTA 1 included 5 subjects aged &lt;12 years of age, hereof 1 subject aged 10 years. The range of weights in the analysis dataset (27 - 154 kg) was also almost completely encompassed by weights in the adult studies (38 - 194 kg), with only 2 patients in the analysis dataset weighing less than 38 kg.

Creatinine clearance was not available for patients from OPERETTA 1 study. BW was correlated with age for patients from OPERETTA 1 while this correlation was not observed for patients from OPERETTA 2, because no patients with weight &lt;50 kg enrolled in this study.

The recently developed PPK model for ocrelizumab IV in adult patients with MS (further called the adult IV model) was used as the initial model. Ocrelizumab clearance comprised of a sum of timeindependent and time-dependent terms, where time-dependent term decreased with time on treatment (exponentially with the decay coefficient kdes).

An external evaluation of the adult IV model utilizing data from OPERETTA 1 and OPERETTA 2 was conducted using goodness of fit (GoF) plots and simulation-based diagnostics. Diagnostic plots revealed a bias in younger children (&lt;14 years), particularly in clearance and volume parameters. This warranted a model extension to account for age-related effects in younger children (Table 1). All the other parameters of the final model were fixed at the values of the adult IV model.

The final model incorporated age-dependent adjustments to CLT, as well as to central and peripheral volumes (Vc and Vp), using piecewise functions relative to a defined age cutoff.

Individual exposures in paediatric patients were predicted based on the final model and compared to adult patients with RMS and PPMS.

## 2.3.3.3. OPERETTA 1

Children and adolescents with RRMS were enrolled in Study WA39085.

The key inclusion criteria were as follows: age at screening between ≥10 and &lt; 18 years. BW ≥ 25 kg.

OPERETTA 1 is an open-label, parallel-group, dose-finding Phase II study to evaluate the safety, tolerability, PK, and pharmacodynamics (PD) of ocrelizumab in children and adolescents aged ≥10 to &lt;18 years with RRMS. This study was designed to determine the appropriate dose of ocrelizumab for pediatric patients with MS, and to explore the relationship between drug exposure and PD (CD19+ Bcell count), safety, and efficacy endpoints.

The study consists of 4 periods, an 8-week eligibility screening period, a 24-week dose exploration period, followed by a 264-week optional ocrelizumab extension (OOE) period, and at least a 48-week safety follow-up (SFU) period from the date of the last infusion (Figure 4). Patients who completed the dose exploration period or the OOE period, or patients who discontinued treatment prematurely for any reason at any time during the study entered the 48-week SFU period.

Two dose levels were initially assessed in the study:

- Cohort 1: A dose of 300 mg ocrelizumab for patients with a BW from ≥25 kg to &lt;40 kg was evaluated. Six patients (F:M 5:1, age 10-12 years, 2 subjects with tanner &lt;2) were enrolled, with 2 patients with a BW from ≥25 kg to ≤35 kg and 4 patients with a BW &gt;35 kg to &lt;40kg.
- Cohort 2: A dose of 600 mg ocrelizumab for patients with a BW ≥40 kg was evaluated. Seventeen patients (F:M 10:7 age 11-17 years) were enrolled, including 5 patients with a BW ≥40 kg, but ≤50 kg. All other patients had a BW &gt;50 kg.

As used for adult patients, the first dose of ocrelizumab was administered as 2 IV infusions of half the dose given 14 days apart (i.e., 2 x 150 mg for a total dose of 300 mg, and 2 x 300 mg for a total dose of 600 mg) to minimize the incidence and severity of infusion related reaction (IRR) upon the first administration of ocrelizumab. Subsequent doses of ocrelizumab (i.e., 300 mg or 600 mg) were administered as single infusions. A visit was required within 2 weeks prior to the planned dosing visit to collect blood samples and patient information to confirm that retreatment criteria were met.

Recruitment into Cohort 2 (patients with a BW ≥40 kg) was much faster than into Cohort 1 (&lt;40 kg), and therefore enrolment into the subsequent OPERETTA 2 study was initially opened only for patients with a minimum BW of first 50 kg and then 40 kg, once enough PK and safety data had been collected in these patient populations. Once Cohort 1 was fully enrolled with the required 6 patients with &lt;40 kg BW, and after the last patient completed the dose exploration period of 24 weeks, the following recommendation was provided by the Internal Monitoring Committee for OPERETTA 2:

- For pediatric patients 10 years of age and older weighing &lt;35 kg, the recommended dosing regimen of ocrelizumab is 300 mg IV infusion every 6 months.
- For pediatric patients 10 years of age and older weighing ≥35 kg, the recommended dosing regimen of ocrelizumab is 600 mg IV infusion every 6 months.

Patients on the 300 mg dose who reached the specified BW cut-off during the study switched to 600 mg at their next infusion visit.

PK samples were drawn on Days 1, 15, 29, 57, 85, 113, 169, 337, 505, and 673 (before administration of premedication prior to the ocrelizumab IV infusion). On infusion visits, two serum samples were collected, one 5-30 minutes prior to the methylprednisolone administration and the second one 30 (±10) minutes following the completion of the infusion of ocrelizumab. At non-infusion visits, samples were collected at any time during the visit. For all participants, a sample was also drawn at delayed dosing days, and withdrawal from treatment visits.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

<!-- image -->

IMC=Internal Monitoring Committee; OOE=Optional Ocrelizumab Extension.

- a Screening for the study could be extended but could not exceed 10 weeks for relevant clinical, administrative, or operational reasons.
- b Eligible patients were allocated into one of two treatment groups depending on their BW at screening. The first dose of ocrelizumab for the 24-week period was administered as two infusions of half the dose 14 days apart. Patients who received a partial or complete dose of ocrelizumab and discontinued from the 24-week period would have entered the SFU period (at least 48 weeks).
- c At Week 24, patients had the opportunity to continue with ocrelizumab treatment by entering a 264-week OOE period. For patients continuing the extension, subsequent doses of ocrelizumab were administered if all re-treatment criteria were met.
- d The treating investigator was required to ensure that patients met criteria for re-treatment with ocrelizumab before each subsequent infusion.
- e It was planned that patients would continue in the OOE period with the dose corresponding to their initial treatment regimen until the appropriate dosing regimen was selected by the IMC. At that time, patients would be assigned to this selected dose level. In a situation where a subsequent dose was higher than the initial treatment regimen, that dose was required to be administered as two infusions of half the dose 14 days apart.
- f Patients completing the 288-week treatment period, or patients who discontinued treatment prematurely for any reason at any time during the study, were expected to enter the SFU period.
- g The IMC were to review all data obtained in each cohort.

## Non-compartmental Analysis

Twenty-one out of the 23 patients enrolled in the study were included in the non-compartmental analysis. Two patients were excluded due to 1) the second infusion administered 126 days after the first infusion instead of the planned 14 days, and 2) a suspected sample mix-up.

In Cohort 1 (n=5), after 300 mg ocrelizumab given as 2 x 150 mg IV infusions 14 days apart to patients with a BW of ≥25 kg to &lt;40 kg, the mean maximum concentration (Cmax), trough concentration (Ctrough) and AUC from time 0 to Week 24 (AUCW1-24) were 121 µg/mL, 0.182 µg/mL and 2680 µg/mL·day, respectively. In Cohort 2 (n=16), after 600 mg ocrelizumab given as 2 x 300 mg IV infusions 14 days apart to patients with a BW ≥40 kg, the mean Cmax, Ctrough and AUCW1-24 were 164 µg/mL, 0.422 µg/mL and 3680 µg/mL·day, respectively. The apparent elimination half-life (mean) was between 18.0 days and 19.8 days across the 2 cohorts (Table 2).

Based on the PK data observed in this study in pediatric patients, two potential dosing regimens were simulated. The first option is a dosing regimen as described in the study protocol and as administered in this study, i.e., a dose of 300 mg ocrelizumab IV for patients &lt;40 kg BW and 600 mg for patients ≥40 kg BW. The second option is a dosing regimen with 300 mg for patients &lt;35 kg BW and 600 mg for patients ≥35 kg BW. Both dosing regimens provided similar exposure compared to the exposure range observed in adult MS patients treated with 600 mg ocrelizumab. The option with 35 kg BW as the cut-off point provides exposure values closer to the upper end of the adult exposure range for the pediatric patients with a BW of 35 to 40 kg (Figure 6 and Figure 7), whereas the cut-point of 40 kg is predicted to provide exposure values closer to the lower end of the adult exposure range (Figure 8 and Figure 9). Given that MS is a progressive, life-long, and debilitating disease, it is considered important to provide good efficacy from the start of treatment to pediatric patients experiencing symptoms of MS very early in their life. In addition, most pediatric MS patients will have a BW above 35 kg. Applying the same dosing regimen of 600 mg for the vast majority of all MS patients including pediatric patients will simplify administration procedures and avoid dosing errors. Therefore, the selected dosing regimen to be assessed in the subsequent efficacy and safety study in pediatric patients is 300 mg ocrelizumab IV for patients &lt;35 kg BW and 600 mg for patients ≥35 kg BW.

## 2.3.3.4. OPERETTA 2

OPERETTA 2 is a randomized, double-blind, double-dummy, parallel-group, multicenter Phase III study to evaluate the safety and efficacy of ocrelizumab administered by IV infusion every 24 weeks compared with fingolimod taken orally (PO) once a day (QD) in children and adolescents aged ≥10 to &lt;18 years with RRMS (see section 2.4.1 for further details).

Participants were randomized 1:1 to treatment with ocrelizumab or fingolimod, stratified by region (United States vs. rest of world (RoW), pre-pubertal status (yes vs. no), and presence of T1 gadolinium (Gd) lesions at baseline (yes vs. no). A total of 93 patients were randomized to ocrelizumab, and 94 patients were randomized to fingolimod. The study consists of an 8-10 weeks screening period, a flexible-duration double-blind treatment period of at least 24 weeks for the last patient randomized, an open label extension (OLE) period (at least 144 weeks), and SFU period of at least 48 weeks.

Patients were due to receive ocrelizumab (or ocrelizumab placebo) by IV infusion every 24 weeks at a dose of 600 mg for patients with a BW ≥35 kg. Patients were due to receive ocrelizumab (or ocrelizumab placebo) by IV infusion every 24 weeks at a dose of 300 mg for patients with a BW &lt;35 kg. However, no participants were enrolled in this weight category, as initially at study start enrolment was restricted to ≥50 kg and then ≥40 kg as no PK and safety data was available yet from OPERETTA 1 at that time for younger patients with lower BW.

The first dose was administered as 2 IV infusions of half the dose given 14 days apart. For the subsequent doses, ocrelizumab (or ocrelizumab placebo) was administered by IV infusion every 24 weeks as a single dose.

In addition to receiving ocrelizumab or ocrelizumab placebo, patients were due to receive fingolimod or fingolimod placebo taken PO QD as per the prescribing information (i.e., 0.25 mg for patients with a BW ≤40 kg or 0.5 mg for patients with a BW &gt;40 kg).

PK samples were collected at baseline, at Week 2, Week 12, every 12 weeks thereafter until Week 96, and at delayed dosing visits. On ocrelizumab infusion days, 2 serum samples were to be collected, one prior to the methylprednisolone administration and one 30 minutes after completion of the ocrelizumab infusion. On visits without ocrelizumab infusion, PK samples could have been collected at any time.

Nonlinear mixed-effects modelling was used to analyse the sparse concentration-time data of ocrelizumab via the population PK approach. Patients who had measurable concentrations of ocrelizumab were included in the PK analysis unless major protocol deviations (MPD) or unavailability of information (e.g., exact blood sampling time) occurred which interfered with PK evaluation (clinical study report (CSR)).

Results up to the CCOD of 9 June 2025 (corresponding to the time after the last patient randomized had completed 24 weeks of treatment) are presented in the OPERETTA 2 Primary CSR.

No NCA of PK data was located for OPERETTA 2. Instead PK parameters were reported in the popPK modelling report 114975. PK parameters were reported (predicted) for both OPERETTA 2 separately and for pooled PK data from OPERETTA 1 and 2. The pooled data serve as the basis for the amended wording on exposure in the paediatric population in section 5.2 of the SmPC (AUC24w = 3130 µg/mL x hr and Cmax = 147 µg/mL). See below.

## 2.3.4. Pharmacodynamics

Both OPERETTA 1 and OPERETTA 2 provide PD data in paediatric RMS patients. CD19+ B -cell count in blood is the PD biomarker for ocrelizumab.

## Mechanism of action

Ocrelizumab is a recombinant humanized monoclonal antibody that selectively depletes cluster of differentiation (CD)20-expressing B cells. CD20 is a cell surface antigen found on pre-B cells, mature and memory B cells but not expressed on lymphoid stem cells and plasma cells. Although ocrelizumab selectively depletes CD20-expressing B cells, the capacity for B-cell reconstitution and pre-existing humoral immunity are preserved. In addition, innate immunity and total T-cell numbers are not affected.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Primary and secondary pharmacology

## OPERETTA 1

Ocrelizumab treatment led to rapid and near-complete B-cell depletion in blood, which was sustained throughout treatment. More B cell repletion between infusions was observed for Cohort 1 in patients &lt;40 kg BW receiving 300 mg ocrelizumab compared to Cohort 2 in patients with a BW ≥40 kg receiving 600 mg ocrelizumab every 24 weeks. B-cell count summary by cohort is discussed below as mean, standard deviation (SD) and median cell count and is presented in Figure 11.

Cohort 1 baseline mean (SD) was 377.00 (207.71), median 287.00 cells/µL and cohort 2 baseline mean was (SD) 263.82 (113.82), median 234.00 cells/µL. At Week 2, prior to receiving the second half of Dose 1 infusion, rapid and near complete depletion of circulating B cells were observed in all patients in both cohorts:

- Cohort 1: mean (SD) 7.8 (11.71), median 2.00 cells/µL; 0% (0 out of 5 patients) repleted
- Cohort 2: mean (SD) 0.67 (0.98), median 0.00 cells/µL; 0% (0 out of 15 patients) repleted

During Weeks 4, 8, 12 and 16, B-cell count remained low, and no B-cell repletion was observed. At Week 24, prior to receiving the second dose of ocrelizumab, some B-cell reconstitution was observed in both cohorts. While in the majority of patients the B-cell reconstitution level remained lower than the repletion threshold, one patient from each cohort (Cohort 1: 1/6 patients, Cohort 2: 1/17 patients) had their B-cell count returned above LLN:

- Cohort 1 Week 24: mean (SD) 133.3 (139.11), median 91.0 cells/µL; 16.7% (1/6 patients) repleted
- Cohort 2 Week 24: mean (SD) 43.9 (54.5), median 9.0 cells/µL; 5.9% (1/17 patients) repleted

## OPERETTA 2

Treatment with ocrelizumab IV led to rapid and sustained near-complete B-cell depletion in blood. In the fingolimod arm, a substantial and persistent reduction of lymphocytes in blood was also observed, which was expected based on the mechanism of action of fingolimod.

## 2.3.5. Exposure-response/safety

The graphical analysis of CD19+ B cell count in blood at Week 24 versus AUC could potentially indicate that lower exposure increases the likelihood of CD19+ B cell repletion before the next subsequent administration of ocrelizumab at Week 24, indicating that it is important to achieve and maintain adequate ocrelizumab exposure in all pediatric patients (Figure 16).
