## 2.3.6. PK/PD modelling

Graphical exploratory analysis indicated no relationship between exposure and efficacy or safety, thus no further modelling was performed.

## 2.3.7. Discussion on clinical pharmacology

Bioanalytical program is considered appropriate.

## Modelling

Appropriate software and estimation methods were utilised for PopPK modelling.

Two IV dose levels were administered, 300 mg and 600 mg, the BW threshold for switching to the higher dose was ≥35 kg for OPERETTA 2 and ≥40 kg for OPERETTA 1. As no subjects treated with ocrelizumab in OPERETTA 2 weighed &lt;50 kg. Thus, no PK data of 600 mg dosing of subjects weighing ≥35 and &lt;40 kg were collected despite the chosen threshold weight of ≥35 kg. 6 subjects weighted &lt; 40 kg, of these 2 subjects weighed &lt;35 kg. 1 subject was aged 10 years and 5 subjects were aged 11 years. External validation of the previously developed adult IV model using the paediatric data indicated the presence of age-related bias. Thus, the adult IV model was used as an initial model for further model development. The final paediatric model fixed all parameters of the adult IV model and included age-adjusted effects on CLT, and piecewise functions were used to add an age effect on VC and VP at a defined cut-off age (estimated to be 14.4 years). Relative standard error (RSE) of the estimated parameters were 34.1% for CLTage, 3.81% for Agecutoff, and 28.8% for Vage. Eta-shrinkage of the final paediatric model were all &gt;20% and epsilon-shrinkage was 28.7%. GoF and pcVPC plots of the final paediatric model indicated that the model was able to fit the PK data reasonably well, also when stratifying by age &lt;12 years. Plots overlaying paediatric and adult predictions indicated similar exposure between the two populations. Graphical exploratory analysis indicated no relationship between exposure and efficacy or safety. Thus, no exposure-response modelling was performed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Pharmacokinetics

To support the study design of OPERETTA 1, the PopPK model built on PK data from adults predicted that a 600-mg ocrelizumab dose in children and adolescent patients with a BW ≥40 kg would result in exposure similar to the exposure range observed in the pivotal adult studies. The dose of 300 mg in patients with a BW from ≥25 kg to &lt;40 kg was predicted to result in exposure below or similar to the adult exposure, even in young children at the very low end of the BW range (i.e., 25 kg) (CSR). Therefore, in OPERETTA 1, children with BW &lt;40 kg were dosed 300 mg and children ≥40 kg were dosed with 600 mg. Rich PK sampling was obtained after the first set of two doses (2 x 150 mg or 2 x 300 mg), thereafter, serum samples were collected just before dosing and just after end of infusion. This is considered sufficient for assessing interindividual variation and obtaining relevant measures of exposure for the first 24 weeks. According to protocol and in compliance with PIP, 6 patients were eligible for the low dose of 300 mg ocrelizumab (five females and one male patient below 40 kg at baseline). Unfortunately, apparent swopping of the pre- and post-dose samples in week 24 precluded one patient to be included in the NCA. As raw PK data from this patient otherwise appeared similar to the other patients, the data was used in the popPK model in the manner which made PK sense. This is supported. Two patients with BW 39kg were observed with substantially lower AUC compared to the rest of the population and mean AUC24w in the adult population. One of these patients was also observed with breakthrough of CD19 cells already on week 12 whereas the other patient showed breakthrough in week 24, just prior to the next dose. These observations support the selection of &lt;35 kg to be the BW eligible for the low dose of 300 mg. One patient in the lowest weight tier (&lt;30 kg) was observed with higher exposure than the other four patients, but still relatively close to mean adult exposure. Whereas another patient two years younger with similar BW at baseline (&lt;30 kg) showed exposure lower than the mean observed AUC24w (3510 µg/mL x hr) in adults. Hence, this one and only patient appears to illustrate the faster clearance in the youngest, since another older patient with similar bodyweight showed higher exposure or this could be just biological variation. Nevertheless, 5 patients with BW below 40 kg and one patient age below 12 year old with BW above 40 kg receiving the 600 mg dose provided richly sampled PK data for the PopPK model, which appeared to describe the data in the youngest reasonably well by incorporating a new age-dependent change in the CLT on top of the established adult model. Of the patients eligible for the 600 mg dose, all but one patient was 14 years old or older. Two patients had exposures clearly higher than the rest of the set. Exposure was still within the observed range in adults and these two patients did not show any exaggerated adverse events. OPERETTA 2 was a randomized, double-blind, double-dummy, parallel-group, multicenter Phase III study to evaluate the safety and efficacy of ocrelizumab administered by IV infusion every 24 weeks compared with fingolimod taken PO QD in children and adolescents aged ≥10 to &lt;18 years with RRMS. 93 patients were randomised to ocrelizumab of which at least one post dose sample was collected to inform the popPK model. Sparse sampling was systematically performed in this study but no noncompartmental analysis was provided. This is acceptable as only pre- and post-dose samples were collected. No patients below 50 kg were enrolled in OPERETTA 2 and only one patient was below 12 years of age. Hence, the lower end of BW and age range is primarily covered by OPERETTA 1. As 93 adolescents were enrolled in the study, this illustrates the rarity of RRMS in this young age group and is accepted. PKs was reported using predicted exposure obtained by modelling. Full graphical comparison of observed and predicted exposure was provided for individual patients. This demonstrates acceptable overlap between observed and predicted concentrations. The model was also used for predicting exposure in segments of BW and age ranges, which also were in line with observed data. The model incorporated age-dependent revisions to the adult model to accommodate the observed faster clearance in the younger patients. Predicted exposure demonstrates that exposure in the adolescent population administered 300 mg or 600 mg with a BW cut-off of 35 kg is within the observed exposure in adults.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Pharmacodynamics

In OPERETTA 1 and 2 onset of CD19+ B cell depletion after ocrelizumab treatment was fast (about 2 weeks) and sustained for the majority of the population. This is consistent with the described mechanism of action and CD19+ is the appropriate PD marker. The frequency of CD19+B cell repletion between doses was low in OPERETTA 2 (6 patients with 9 repletion events during the trial). In OPERETTA 1 CD19+ B cell repletion occurred at a higher frequency in the low-dose (300 mg) cohort 1 (n=1 patient, 16.7%), and was sustained at several visits, compared to cohort 2 (n=1 patient, 5.9%) treated with adult doses of 600 mg (OPERETTA 1). It was noted that higher CD19 cell levels (i.e. CD19 cells [&gt; 10 cells/µL] but below the limit of a repletion event) appear to be more frequent in cohort 1 (6/6 patients) than cohort 2 (11/17 patients). CD19 B cell repletion events only occurred in children aged 11-14 years-old (most of them males). The distribution of CD19 B cell repletion events is therefore inconsistent with the overall trial population in OPERETTA 2 (69% females, mean age 15 years old). Bearing in mind the very low number of events overall (8 patients in total across OPERETTA 1+2 of which 7 were on the 600 mg dose) this observation should be interpreted with some caution. Repletion events were rare in OPERETTA 2 (6.5%), in line with the adult population (~6% from ocrelizumab adult dossier). The events were not generally sustained during the trial, and ongoing treatment appears to have resulted in more profound and sustained B cell depletion, which is biologically plausible and in line with observations in adults. As CD19 B cells is a PD marker reflecting target engagement, and not a well-established surrogate marker of clinical efficacy, the clinical impact of these singular repletion events in young patients is uncertain.

The longitudinal pattern of CD19 counts is similar in patients from OPERETTA 2 with and without relapses. There are cases in which a timely association between CD19+ B cell breakthrough and/or repletion and radiological/clinical relapse is observed. It is noted that these relapses mainly occur before week 48 and are singular. Considering the incomplete understanding of the inflammatory pathophysiology underlying RMS relapses and disease activity, these observations are not clearly consistent with systematic signs of inflammatory activity following singular CD19 repletion events or a minor degree of CD19 breakthrough. The exact degree of CD19 B cell depletion which is needed for clinical efficacy remains uncertain. It is noted that the mean levels of CD19 B cells in OPERETTA 2 are similar to findings in the original, adult dossier (mean of 26.16±79.59 cells/µL) even though baseline, normal levels of CD19 B cells are higher in children than in adults. In OPERETTA 1 (cohort 2, dosed with 600 mg) the mean level is higher at week 24 than expected compared to adults (43.9 ± 54.5 cells/µL). Given the favourable outcomes in OPERETTA 1 (no clinical relapses), the low number of patients (n=17) and a low median value (9 cells/µL in OPERETTA 1 compared to 2 cells/µL in OPERETTA 2, both reflecting profound depletion), the clinical significance of this finding cannot be established. Bearing in mind the paucity of the data, the exact weight cut-off for the posology should be driven by predicted exposure (PK/PD modelling) and clinical data rather than singular B cell repletion events.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 2.3.8. Conclusions on clinical pharmacology

The PK and PD program is sufficient for describing exposure in the intended population and to be in compliance with the PIP.

## 2.4. Clinical efficacy

## 2.4.1. Main study OPERETTA 2 (study WN42096)

## Methods

Phase III multicenter, randomized, double-blind, double-dummy, parallel-group, non-inferiority study to evaluate safety and efficacy of ocrelizumab in comparison with fingolimod in children and adolescents with RRMS.

The study consisted of the following study periods:

- Screening period of maximum 10 weeks. Patients may qualify for two re-screening opportunities.
- DBP with flexible duration, at least 24 weeks for the last patient randomized;
- OOLE period (at least 144 weeks);
- SFU period (at least 48 weeks);
- B-cell monitoring will continue for patients with peripheral blood B cells still depleted after the SFU period until repletion to baseline or age-specific lower limit of normal (LLN).

The total length of the study, from screening to the end of the SFU, varies from patient to patient because of the flexible duration of the DBP (minimum of 24 weeks), which depends on each patient's time point of inclusion during the enrollment period.

Figure 18: Study Design

<!-- image -->

b Following database lock of the DBP, patients continued to receive blinded treatment, until the primary analysis was completed and the decision to start the OLE phase was communicated to the investigators by the Sponsor. The OLE period will last for at least 144

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

weeks and may be extended until the patient turns 18 years old (or as required per local regulation) or until commercial ocrelizumab is approved for children and available in the country for these patients, whichever occurs first

c In the OLE, brain MRI scans will be performed 24 weeks after Dose 1 and yearly thereafter.

d SFU period will start after the DBP or the OLE period and last for at least 48 weeks, counting from the final ocrelizumab dose received. Patients discontinuing treatment during the DBP were planned to be followed within the DBP for safety and efficacy, in line with the ICH E9 (R1) addendum (ICH 2020). Patients randomized to receive ocrelizumab, who discontinued treatment during the DBP, but did not yet reach 8 weeks after last dose of ocrelizumab at the time of the end of the DBP, will be followed for the remainder of the required 48-week SFU period.

e If after the SFU, the peripheral blood B-cells remain depleted, patients will continue to be monitored at 24-week intervals until the B-cell count has returned to the baseline value or to the age-specific LLN range (whichever is lower)

## Rationale for Flexible Duration of the Double-Blind Treatment Period

Pediatric trials can take an inordinate amount of time to complete because much of the time is spent in the enrollment phase. A fixed duration study design, where all patients are followed up for the same amount of time, will require both a large sample size and consequently longer total study duration. In a flexible study duration setting, where all patients stay in the study until the primary data read-out (from double-blind, double-dummy comparative treatment period), the trial will gain more statistical power to test the treatment effect, and thus require a smaller sample size and shorter total study duration. A shorter trial ensures that the research question the trial has been designed to test can be answered quicker and new treatments could be offered to patients sooner. Furthermore, a flexible duration study design will enable collection of long-term safety and efficacy data from individual patients. From a patient perspective, the only difference between a flexible duration design and a fixed duration design is in the duration of the double-blind, double-dummy comparative treatment period, which will last longer for most of the patients (i.e., up to 3 years for the first recruited patients in case of a 30-month recruitment period). However, since the study drug, ocrelizumab, is only administered every 24 weeks, and the active comparator, fingolimod, is an oral daily treatment, the inconvenience for patients resulting from a longer double-dummy period duration is anticipated to be minor.

## Schedule of activities

All patients will follow the same schedule of activities regardless of their treatment assignment. To confirm re-treatment criteria are met, a visit will be required within 2 weeks prior to any planned administration of IV ocrelizumab/ocrelizumab placebo to collect blood samples and patient information.

Screening visit which includes informed consent, review of eligibility, physical examination and medical history, wrist/hand X ray, brain MRI, ophthalmologic and neurologic evaluation, collection of trial data (i.e. Expanded Disability Status Scale (EDSS) scoring) and biomarkers, including comprehensive biochemical evaluation with relevant tests for virus titers, cell counts, myelin oligodendrocyte glycoprotein (MOG) antibodies, thyroid function, aquaporin-4 (AQP-4) antibody test, urin- and pregnancy test, electrocardiogram (ECG).

Further visits included a baseline visit in which the first dose of study drug was given, eligibility was reconfirmed, medical history, physical and neurological exam including clinical trial scores such as EDSS. Telephone follow-up calls 24h after each infusion was done to assess infusion related reaction (IRR).

Treatment visits (ocrelizumab/ocrelizumab placebo) followed the dosing regimen recommended in the protocol (every 24 weeks apart from 1st dose). Apart from ocrelizumab/ocrelizumab placebo infusion the patient was assessed with physical and neurological exam, brain MRI without gadolinium, EDSS, symbol digit modality test (SDMT) and other clinical scores, medical history including adverse events, ADAs, routine laboratory analyses including urinalysis and pregnancy test.

Follow-up visits assessed for clinical relapses, adverse events and laboratory analyses and included an on-site visit 12 weeks from the latest ocrelizumab/ocrelizumab placebo dose, supplemented with monthly, structured telephone interviews and unscheduled visits i.e. for clinical relapse confirmation (EDSS score within 7 days of suspected relapse).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

2 weeks before re-treatment with ocrelizumab/ocrelizumab placebo the patient had a visit to assess criteria for re-treatment (previous grade 4 IRR events, anaphylactic reactions/allergies, absence of infection, assessment of weight and concomitant medications, pregnancy and acceptable neutrophil counts, CD4 levels and IgG levels as assessed by the investigator).

## Neuroimaging

Brain MRI is performed with and without Gd-contrast after screening and week 12. After this brain MRIs are performed without Gd-contrast at week 24 and every 24 weeks thereafter. MRI scans are obtained in patients withdrawing from treatment if not performed within the past 4 weeks before the withdrawal visit.

The quality of each MRI scan will be assessed by the central reading center who may request re-scans if needed. After completion of the quality check, all MRI scans will be analyzed by the central reading vendor (blinded). Scans will be performed by trained and certified blinded MRI technicians.

During the DBP, the central blinded MRI reading center will inform the treating investigator whenever there is a pre-defined deterioration on the MRIs using the following process:

- For any given patients with at least five new or enlarging T2 lesions on any MRI scans from Week 48 inclusive and in comparison to the most recent prior scan.
- As part of this notification process, the treating investigator will be provided with the baseline total count of T2 lesions and the count of new or enlarging T2 lesions from the MRI having triggered the notification, as well as both related MRI images (including Gd T1 lesion count at baseline). However, treatment allocation will remain blinded.

Thereafter, the blinding rules regarding the MRI review will be reinstated and notifications will be sent only in case of a scan showing at least five new or enlarging T2 lesions on any MRI scans in comparison with the most recent prior scan.

During the DBP and up to the primary CSR CCOD, MRI notifications were sent for 7 patients, some of them having been triggered at more than 1 visit, for a total of 10 notifications (fingolimod arm only).

## Central laboratory assessments

On infusions visits, all samples must be taken prior to the methylprednisolone infusion. During the double-blind period, some laboratory parameters that could reveal patient's allocation to study treatment, such as flow cytometry cell counts, white blood count (WBC) count, lymphocyte counts (including CD4 lymphocytes), Ig levels, and liver function tests, will be blinded. To ensure patients' safety in the study and to allow for assessments of the re-treatment criteria, a central laboratory will provide study investigators and Medical Monitors with reflex messages triggered by critical blinded laboratory results. Investigators notified of their patient's critical laboratory test results will be instructed to suspend further treatment with study drug until the patient becomes eligible for retreatment.

During the DBP and up to the primary CSR CCOD, unblinded laboratory notifications were sent for 27 patients, some of them having been triggered at more than one visit, for a total of 70 notifications. A listing of Critical Laboratory Notifications is submitted. To summarize:

- A total of 16 unblinded ALT (alanine aminotransferase) laboratory notifications were sent for 9 patients (3 patients in the OCR arm and 6 patients in the FINGO arm).
- A total of 5 unblinded AST (aspartate aminotransferase) laboratory notifications were sent for 5 patients (3 patients in the OCR arm and 2 patients in the FINGO arm).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- A total of 9 unblinded lymphocyte laboratory notifications were sent for 6 patients, all in the FINGO arm.
- A total of 37 unblinded WBC laboratory notifications were sent for 13 patients (1 patient in the OCR arm and 12 patients in the FINGO arm).
- No notifications were sent for gamma-glutamyl transferase (GGT), CD4, and IgG parameters.

## Study participants

Patients must have met the following criteria for study entry:

- Informed consent for study participation signed by the parents or a legal guardian, with patient assent obtained verbally and when possible, in writing, from all pediatric patients old enough to fully comprehend the assent document prior to any study-specific screening procedures, as per local requirements;
- Age between ≥ 10 to &lt; 18 years at randomization.

Patients must have met the following criteria related to pediatric MS for study entry:

- Diagnosis of RRMS in accordance with the International Pediatric Multiple Sclerosis Study Group criteria for pediatric MS, Version 2012, or McDonald criteria 2017 (or the most current revision of the International Pediatric Multiple Sclerosis Study Group criteria or McDonald criteria at the time of study start);
- Confirmation of the diagnosis of pediatric RRMS by the Independent Pediatric MS Review Committee prior to randomization;
- EDSS at screening: 0-5.5, both inclusive;
- Disease activity, as defined by the following:

Note: A relapse or an attack is a monophasic clinical episode with patient-reported symptoms and objective findings typical of multiple sclerosis, reflecting a focal or multifocal inflammatory demyelinating event in the CNS, developing acutely or sub-acutely, with duration of at least 24 hours, with or without recovery, and in the absence of fever or infection. \"Attack,\" \"relapse,\" and \"exacerbation\" are synonyms (Thompson et al. 2018).

- For all countries except Germany: At least one relapse during the year prior to screening or two relapses in the previous two years prior to screening or evidence of at least one Gdenhancing lesion on MRI within 6 months prior to randomization (including screening MRI);
- For Germany: Highly active disease despite a full and adequate course of treatment with at least one DMT, or patients with rapidly evolving severe RRMS defined by two or more disabling relapses in one year, and with at least one Gd-enhancing lesion on brain MRI or a significant increase in T2 lesion load as compared to a previous recent MRI.

Exclusion Criteria Related to General Health

- Known presence or suspicion (based on clinical or laboratory parameters) of other neurologic disorders that may mimic MS, including, but not limited to, ADEM, neuromyelitis optica or neuromyelitis optica spectrum disorders; and any neurological (other than MS), somatic, or metabolic condition that could interfere with brain function or normal cognitive or neurological development
- In case of an ADEM like appearance of the first MS relapse, a second relapse with clear MS like features is required.
- Patients that were AQP-4 and/or MOG antibody positive at screening;

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Clinical or laboratory findings at first presentation not typically for MS, such as signs of infection; signs of encephalopathy such as confusion, convulsion, reduced state of consciousness.
- Abnormal findings in the cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) at first MS presentation. Protein &gt; 100 mg/dL. Pleocytosis &gt; 50 cells/mm3. Presence of neutrophils or eosinophils above the normal reference range per local laboratory, or atypical cells.

Note: CSF sampling is not mandated at screening and may be performed at investigator discretion to confirm diagnosis of pediatric RRMS.

- Atypical MRI findings: ADEM-like presentation of lesions; lesions in atypical location for MS; bilateral optic neuritis; extensive spinal cord lesions (≥3 spinal segments)

## Exclusion Criteria Related to Medications

- Treatment with any investigational agent within 24 weeks of screening or five half-lives, whichever is longer (or longer if indicated by the PD action of the drug);
- Previous treatment with B-cell-targeted therapies (i.e., rituximab, ocrelizumab, obinutuzumab, atacicept, belimumab, or ofatumumab);
- Any previous treatment with alemtuzumab, anti-CD4, cladribine, mitoxantrone, daclizumab, laquinimod, total body irradiation, or bone marrow transplantation;
- Previous treatment with any other immunomodulatory or immunosuppressive medication not already listed above without appropriate washout as described in the applicable local label (washout to be completed prior to baseline);
- Treatment with natalizumab within 12 months prior to randomization;
- Treatment with teriflunomide within 24 weeks prior to treatment allocation of patients who have not completed an accelerated washout procedure or treatment with teriflunomide within 4 weeks prior to treatment allocation of patients who have completed an accelerated washout procedure;
- Previous treatment with fingolimod;
- Treatment with any other S1P receptor modulator (e.g., BAF312/siponimod) within 24 weeks prior to treatment allocation;
- Treatment with dimethyl fumarate within 4 weeks prior to treatment allocation and lymphocyte count &lt; LLN for age- and sex-specific reference range;
- Treatment with IV immunoglobulin within 12 weeks prior to treatment allocation;
- Treatment with plasmapheresis within 4 weeks prior to treatment allocation;
- Completion of systemic corticosteroid therapy within 7 days prior to treatment allocation.

## Treatments

Patients were randomly assigned in a 1:1 ratio to one of two treatment arms:

- Group A (OCRELIZUMAB arm): ocrelizumab IV plus fingolimod PO placebo
- Group B (FINGO arm): fingolimod PO plus ocrelizumab IV placebo

For patients with a BW ≥ 35 kg: The planned dose was 600 mg ocrelizumab or ocrelizumab placebo administered by IV infusion every 24 weeks. The first dose of ocrelizumab or ocrelizumab placebo was to be administered as two 300 mg IV infusions given 14 days apart. For the subsequent doses, ocrelizumab or ocrelizumab placebo was to be administered as a single dose of 600 mg IV infusion every 24 weeks. The 300 mg infusions of ocrelizumab or ocrelizumab placebo (Dose 1) was to be given over approximately 2.5 hours, and each 600 mg ocrelizumab or ocrelizumab.

Likewise, for patients with a BW of &gt; 40 kg the planned dose of fingolimod or fingolimod placebo was 0.5 mg PO QD once daily.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Ocrelizumab placebo was supplied in the identical vial with the same fill volume and formulation components (20 mM sodium acetate at pH 5.3, with 106 mM trehalose dihydrate and 0.02% polysorbate 20), but did not contain ocrelizumab. Fingolimod capsules were modified by overencapsulating. Fingolimod placebo capsules were presented in identical form and packaging as the active capsules, but did not contain fingolimod. Compliance with ocrelizumab/ocrelizumab placebo administration was assessed by maintaining adequate drug preparation and dispensing records. Patient compliance of daily administration of fingolimod/fingolimod placebo will be monitored via reconciliation of medications returned to site at each visit.

There is consensus in the MS community that the use of placebo in Phase III studies of pediatric patients with MS is ethically indefensible, due to the availability of effective therapies approved in adult patients with MS used off-label in the pediatric population. Furthermore, the registration of fingolimod on the basis of level 1A evidence demonstration of its superiority vs. interferon in pediatric MS (Chitnis et al. 2018) has effectively closed the option to use placebo as comparator in pediatric MS studies, making fingolimod a natural active comparator of choice for future development in this indication.

## Pre-medications (all patients)

The non-investigational medicinal products for this study included pre-medications for ocrelizumab or ocrelizumab placebo infusion, as follows:

- Mandatory methylprednisolone (or an equivalent)
- Mandatory antihistamine drug (e.g., diphenhydramine)
- Recommended oral analgesic/antipyretic (e.g., acetaminophen)

## Treatment for Symptoms of Multiple Sclerosis:

The treating investigator should attempt to maintain therapies or treatments for symptoms related to MS (e.g., walking ability, spasticity, incontinence, pain, fatigue) reasonably constant throughout the study.

## Treatment of Relapses:

Patients who experience a relapse during the study may receive treatment with IV or oral corticosteroids, if judged to be clinically appropriate by the investigator. The following standardized treatment regimen may be used as warranted: methylprednisolone IV 30 mg/kg/day (maximum daily dose of 1000 mg) for 3-5 days. In addition, at the discretion of the investigator, corticosteroids may be stopped abruptly or tapered over a maximum of 10 days. Such patients should not discontinue the treatment solely based on the occurrence of a relapse, unless the investigator considers him or her to have met the criteria for withdrawal.

## Objectives, outcomes and estimands

## Primary objective, estimand and outcome

The primary objective aims to demonstrate non-inferiority of ocrelizumab compared with fingolimod.

## Estimand for the primary objective

- Population: patients with RRMS aged between ≥ 10 and &lt; 18 years. The analysis population will consist of all randomized patients (approximately 171 patients), with patients grouped according to their assigned treatment
- Endpoint: the number of protocol-defined relapses during the DBP from when patients initiated study treatment
- Intercurrent events and handling strategies:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- o Premature discontinuation from treatment: hypothetical strategy, only data collected before this intercurrent event will be included in the analysis, with the exception of subsequent data used to confirm protocol-defined relapse; assuming the future event rate behave as observed in the period before withdrawing from treatment, hence no imputation will be performed.
- o Initiation of another MS DMT medication: hypothetical strategy, only data collected before this intercurrent event will be included in the analysis and no imputation will be performed.
- Population-level summary: rate ratio of protocol-defined annualized relapsed rate (ARR)

The comparison of interest is the difference of protocol-defined ARR, as measured through the rate ratio in counts during the DBP.

## Definition of protocol-defined relapse

All patients complaining of new or worsening neurologic symptoms defined at a site visit, mobile nursing visit, or over the phone should be referred to the examining investigator within 7 days of the onset of the relapse, to assess the functional system (FS) score and EDSS independently, to allow confirmation as to whether or not the clinical relapse meets the criteria for protocol-defined relapse.

A protocol-defined relapse is defined as the occurrence of new or worsening neurological symptoms attributable to MS and immediately preceded by a relatively stable or improving neurological state for at least 30 days. Symptoms must persist for at least 24 hours and should not be attributable to confounding clinical factors (e.g., fever, infection, injury, or adverse reactions to concomitant medications). The new or worsening neurological symptoms must be accompanied by objective neurological worsening consistent with an increase of at least half a step on the EDSS score, or 2 points on one of the appropriate FS scores, or 1 point on two or more of the appropriate FS scores. The change must affect the selected FS (i.e., Pyramidal, Ambulation, Cerebellar, Brainstem, Sensory, or Visual). Episodic spasms, sexual dysfunction, fatigue, mood change, or bladder or bowel urgency or incontinence will not suffice to establish the diagnosis of a relapse.

## Kurtzke Expanded Disability Status Scale (Examining Investigator or Rater)

The EDSS is a disability scale that ranges in 0.5-point steps from 0 (normal) to 10 (death). The EDSS is based on a standard neurologic examination, incorporating seven functional systems (pyramidal, cerebellar, brainstem, sensory, bowel and bladder, visual, and cerebral [or mental]) and ambulation rated and scored as an FS. Each FS is an ordinal clinical rating scale ranging from 0-5 or 6. These ratings are then used in conjunction with observations and information concerning ambulation and use of assistive devices to determine the EDSS score.

Note: determination of fatigue and sexual dysfunction scores is optional. EDSS will be administered by the examining investigator (rater), who will assess EDSS scores independently, at the timepoints indicated in the schedule of activities. The examining investigator (rater) should not consult prior EDSS/FS scores when performing the current EDSS/FS assessments. Results of the neurologic examination (or anything related to patient health) and EDSS scores will not be shared between the treating and examining investigator.

## Secondary objectives, estimands and outcomes

- To demonstrate non-inferiority of ocrelizumab compared with fingolimod based on the number of new or enlarging T2-hyperintense lesions (T2 lesions) as detected by brain MRI during the DBP.
- To demonstrate the superiority of ocrelizumab versus fingolimod on the basis of the following endpoints:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Number of new or enlarging T2 lesions during the DBP (week 48 and 96)
- Number of T1 Gd lesions at Week 12
- Protocol-defined ARR during the DBP (48 and 96 weeks)
- To demonstrate the non-inferiority of ocrelizumab versus fingolimod on the basis of the following endpoints:
- ARR for all patients randomised for at least 48 weeks and 96 weeks
- ARR by 48 weeks and 96 weeks
- Number of new or enlarging T2 lesions as detected by brain MRI at week 48 and week 96

## Estimand for the first secondary objective (non-inferiority)

- Population: as defined for the primary estimand
- Endpoint: number of new or enlarging T2 hyperintense lesions as detected by brain MRI during the DBP.
- Intercurrent events and handling strategies: as defined for the primary estimand
- Population-level summary: rate ratio in annualized rate of new or enlarging T2 hyperintense lesions

## Estimand for the second secondary objective (superiority)

- Population: as defined for the primary estimand
- Endpoint:
- o Number of new or enlarging T2 hyperintense lesions as detected by brain MRI during the DBP
- o Number of T1 Gd lesions at Week 12
- o Protocol-defined ARR during the DBP
- Intercurrent events and handling strategies: as defined for the primary estimand
- Population-level summary:
- o Rate ratio of annualized rate of new or newly enlarged T2 hyperintense lesions
- o Rate ratio of Gd-enhancing T1 lesions per scan
- o Rate ratio of protocol-defined ARR

## Estimands for the secondary objective as required by the PDCO

- Population: patients randomized for at least 48 weeks from the date of randomization to the date of the primary analysis
- Endpoints:
- o Protocol-defined ARR during the DBP from when patients having initiated study treatment
- o Protocol-defined ARR by 48 weeks from when patients having initiated study treatment
- Intercurrent events and handling strategies: as defined above
- Population-level summary: rate ratio of protocol-defined ARR
- Population: patients randomized for at least 96 weeks from the date of randomization to the date of the primary analysis
- Endpoints:
- o Protocol-defined ARR during the DBP from when patients having initiated study treatment
- o Protocol-defined ARR by 96 weeks from when patients having initiated study treatment
- Intercurrent events and handling strategies: as defined above
- Population-level summary: rate ratio of protocol-defined ARR
- Population: all randomized patients

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Endpoint:
- o Number of new or enlarging T2-hyperintense lesions (T2 lesions) as detected by brain MRI at Week 48
- Intercurrent events and handling strategies: as defined above
- Population-level summary: Rate ratio of annualized rate of new or newly enlarged T2 lesions.
- Population: all randomized patients
- Endpoint:
- o Number of new or enlarging T2-hyperintense lesions (T2 lesions) as detected by brain MRI at Week 96
- Intercurrent events and handling strategies: as defined above
- Population-level summary: Rate ratio of annualized rate of new or newly enlarged T2 lesions.

## Exploratory efficacy objectives and endpoints

The exploratory efficacy objective for this study is to evaluate the efficacy of ocrelizumab compared with fingolimod on the basis of the following endpoints (only the relevant ones are included here):

- Time to the first relapse (Kaplan-Meier proportion of relapse-free patients)
- Time to 12 weeks and 24 weeks confirmed disability progression over the treatment period.
- Change in patient- and caregiver-reported Pediatric Quality of Life Inventory™(PedsQL™) Generic Core Scale, Version 4.0, from baseline to Weeks 48 and 96
- Change in patient-reported fatigue, as measured by the PedsQL Multidimensional Fatigue Scale (domain scores and total score) from baseline to Week 48 and 96
- Change in the SDMT from baseline to Weeks 48 and 96

## Other exploratory objectives and endpoints

The immunogenicity objective for this study is to evaluate the immune response to ocrelizumab on the basis of the following endpoint: prevalence of ADAs at baseline and incidence of ADAs during the study

The relationship between ADA status and PK, PD, efficacy, and safety may also be explored.

The immunogenicity analysis population consisted of all patients with at least one ADA assessment. Patients were grouped according to treatment received or, if no treatment was received prior to study discontinuation, according to treatment assigned. When determining post-baseline incidence, patients were considered ADA-positive if they were ADA-negative or had missing data at baseline but developed an ADA response following study drug exposure (treatment induced ADA response), or if they were ADA positive at baseline and the titer of one or more postbaseline samples was at least 0.60 titer unit greater than the titer of the baseline sample (treatment-enhanced ADA response). Patients were considered ADA negative if they were ADA negative or had missing data at baseline and all postbaseline samples were negative, or if they were ADA-positive at baseline, but did not have any postbaseline samples with a titer that was at least 0.60 titer unit greater than the titer of the baseline sample (treatment unaffected).

The exploratory biomarker objective for this study is to identify and/or evaluate biomarkers that are predictive of response to ocrelizumab (i.e., predictive biomarkers), are early surrogates of efficacy, are associated with progression to a more severe disease state (i.e., prognostic biomarkers), are associated with acquired resistance to ocrelizumab, are associated with susceptibility to developing adverse events or can lead to improved adverse event monitoring or investigation (i.e., safety biomarkers), can provide evidence of ocrelizumab activity (i.e., PD biomarkers), or can increase the knowledge and understanding of disease biology and drug safety on the basis of the following endpoints:

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Level of neurofilament light (NfL) before and after ocrelizumab dosing at indicated timepoints
- Relationship between biomarkers in serum and efficacy, safety, PK, immunogenicity, or other biomarker endpoints

## Sample size

The sample size for this study was estimated based on data from ocrelizumab Phase III studies in adults with relapsing multiple sclerosis (Study WA21092 OPERA I [NCT01247324] and Study WA21093 OPERA II [NCT01412333]) and from a fingolimod Phase III study in pediatric patients with MS (PARADIGMS [NCT01892722]). The protocol-defined ARR in pediatric patients with MS treated with fingolimod is 0.12 (Chitnis et al. 2018). Assuming patients treated with ocrelizumab have the same ARR of 0.12 and a dispersion parameter of 1, a sample size of 171 patients in total (1:1 randomization allocation) is required for a non-inferiority margin of 3 at a power of 80% and a one-sided type I error rate of 2.5%, given a recruitment period of around 30 months, flexible duration of minimum follow up of 24 weeks and a drop-out rate of 3% per year. Among the 171 patients, 145 are expected to have been randomized for at least 48 weeks, and 71 for at least 96 weeks, ensuring that a substantial number of patients will have long follow-up to support the efficacy analysis. In reality, 187 patients, arm A 93, Arm B 94 were enrolled and randomized.

## Randomisation

Randomization will occur in a 1:1 ratio using a permuted-block randomization method to ensure a balanced assignment to each treatment arm.

Randomization will be stratified according to the following factors:

- Region (U.S. or RoW)
- Pre-pubertal status (defined as Tanner stage score less than 2) (yes vs. no)
- T1 Gd lesions at baseline using screening MRI (yes or no)

## Blinding (masking)

This study was performed in a double-blind, double-dummy fashion. Randomization and blinding were employed to minimize bias in treatment assignment and to provide the basis for valid statistical inference. The randomization list was blinded and remained blinded until the end of the DBP.

Patients, parents/legal guardians, investigators, and the Sponsor remained blinded to the original (randomized) treatment allocation of all patients until the primary analysis was completed and the primary results were available and communicated by the Sponsor.

This was an assessor blinded study. To prevent potential unblinding of the assigned arm during the DBP as a result of adverse events (AEs) or changes to laboratory results, several additional measures were implemented until the time of the primary analysis, including a \"dual assessor approach\" (i.e., two blinded investigators per site: a treating investigator and an examining investigator) and an independent physician for the supervision of the first administration of study treatments including restart of treatment and dose changes. These roles were performed by three different physicians/health care practitioners. These physicians should keep the same predefined role during the double-blind period for all patients enrolled at their site (refer to the study site blinding plan for more details). Whenever possible, the same person should perform the examination for the full study duration. Patients and parents/legal guardians will be instructed not to discuss any symptoms related to the study treatment with the examining investigator; the examining investigator should remind the patient at the start of the examination. In addition, only the local radiologist/technician assigned to this study had access to the MRI scans post-randomization. To protect blinding, the treating investigator was not to review the MRI scans obtained after randomization unless a safety concern arose. Investigators will be notified of their patient's abnormal laboratory. The reflex messages from the central laboratory, together with non-blinded laboratory results, should be carefully reviewed by the treating investigator before continuing with study treatment.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Statistical methods

All efficacy endpoints will be analyzed using the full analysis set including all randomised patients, with patients grouped according to their randomised (assigned) treatment.

## Hypotheses and multiplicity control

The null hypothesis for the primary analysis will be tested at the 0.025 level (one-sided test) with a non-inferiority margin of 3:

- H0 (null hypothesis): protocol-defined ARR is higher in patients treated with ocrelizumab compared to patients treated with fingolimod, i.e., ocrelizumab is inferior to fingolimod in protocol-defined ARR
- H1 (alternative hypothesis): protocol-defined ARR is not higher in patients treated with ocrelizumab compared to patients treated with fingolimod.

The non-inferiority margin of 3 can be justified using the lower bound of a 95% confidence interval (CI) from the PARADIGMS study. In a non-inferiority trial, a larger non-inferiority margin allows for a reduced sample size. Given the low incidence of pediatric MS and as a rare disease, this would increase the feasibility of this study (Chitnis et al. 2018). If the upper bound of the 95% CI of the rate ratio of ARR is smaller than the non-inferiority margin of 3, non-inferiority will be concluded.

The secondary efficacy estimands (refer to objectives) will be tested according to the hierarchical order listed below, if the primary estimand and each preceding estimand have reached the significance level of 0.05:

1. Non-interiority of ocrelizumab compare with fingolimod based on the number of new or enlarging T2-hyperintense lesions as detected by brain MRI during the DBP with a non-inferiority margin of 1.27. A margin of 1.27 represents that ocrelizumab preserves 50% of the treatment effect. If the upper bound of the 95% CI of the rate ratio of annualized rate of new or enlarging T2 lesions is smaller than the non-inferiority margin of 1.27, non-inferiority will be concluded.

Superiority of ocrelizumab vs fingolimod will be tested in the hierarchical order listed below, if all preceding estimands have reached the significance level of 0.05:

2. Number of new or enlarging T2 lesions during the DBP
3. Number of T1 Gd lesions at Week 12
4. Protocol-defined ARR during the DBP.
5. PDCO specific secondary endpoints for non-inferiority in the following order: ARR for all patients randomised for at least 48 weeks, ARR for all patients randomised for at least 96 weeks, ARR by 48 weeks, ARR by 96 weeks, new or enlarging T2 lesions as detected by brain MRI at week 48 and new or enlarging T2 lesions as detected by brain MRI at week 96

All comparisons (non-inferiority and superiority tests) will be made in the hypothetical situation that patients neither withdraw from the study treatment nor start another MS DMT.

## Statistical model

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A negative binomial model was fitted to the total count during the DBP. The mean function of the model was parameterized using the log link function and the linear predictor included log-transformed exposure for each patient as an offset (except the endpoint of T1 Gd lesions at Week 12), and the assigned treatment and the randomization stratification factors as independent variables. The statistical significance of the estimated ratio of means was calculated by using the Wald test.

## Sensitivity and supplemental analyses

To assess the robustness of the estimated treatment effect to the proposed missing data handling strategy for missing data due to early treatment discontinuation, a tipping point analysis will be performed. Missing data from each treatment arm will be imputed based on the \"missing at random\" assumption, and then a penalty will be added to the imputed data in the respective arm in the following steps.

1. Predict the event rate for each patient based on their treatment arm and stratification factors using the negative binomial model.
2. Apply a penalty by multiplying the event rate by a parameter ranging from 2 to 10 in increments of 1.
3. Simulate the number of relapses patients would have experienced during the unobserved period (from the time of treatment withdrawal until CCOD) for patients who withdrew treatment early using the penalized event rate and a Poisson process for each simulation.
4. Repeat the simulation 100 times to account for variability.

Supplemental analyses:

- 1) The primary estimand will be repeated but using a treatment policy strategy for the intercurrent event of treatment discontinuation to estimate the treatment effect of ocrelizumab versus fingolimod regardless of adherence to the randomised treatment. All data collected during the DBP will be included in the analysis. For premature study discontinuation, multiple imputation of missing data based on trajectories from patients within the same treatment arm who withdraw from treatment but remain in the study will be performed.
- 2) The primary estimand will be repeated with treatment effect being estimated and adjusted for prespecified stratification factors and neurological stability for at least 30 days [yes vs no]. Patients not neurologic stable is defined as patients experiencing relapse between 30 days prior to screening and the date of randomization. Relapse occurred prior to screening is collected in eCRF-MS History page. Patients will be considered as not neurologic stable if the end date of most recent relapse is between 30 days prior to screening and the date of randomization. If the end date is missing, the patient would be considered as not neurological stable.
- 3) Excluding patients who did not meet the exclusion criteria or did not meet the eligibility criteria at baseline

## Subgroups

The primary and key secondary efficacy estimands were presented for the following subgroups:

- Stratification factor: region (RoW vs. U.S.);
- Stratification factor: pre-pubertal (Tanner Stage &lt; 2) vs. pubertal (Tanner Stage ≥ 2);
- Stratification factor: presence of T1 Gd lesions at baseline (yes vs. no);
- Demography: baseline weight (&lt; 35 kg vs. ≥35 kg);
- Demography: baseline age (&lt;14 years old vs. ≥14 years old);
- Time of enrollment: early enrollees (i.e., randomized 1 year prior to CCOD: on/before 09 June 2024) vs. late enrollees (randomized within a year of CCOD: after 09 June 2024);

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- Wash out period of prior corticosteroids use: &gt;30 days prior to randomization vs. ≤30 days prior to randomization;
- MS disease characteristics: DMT prior to enrollment yes vs. no.

## Results

## Participant flow

A total of 254 patients were screened for the study, and 66 patients failed screening, based on information collected on the Interactive X Response System. A flow chart of patient disposition is shown in Figure 19.

Figure 19: Flow chart of patient disposition

<!-- image -->

One of 188 randomizations was an erroneous re-randomization of the same patient; therefore, the number of patients randomized was 187.

Patients who prematurely discontinued from study treatment during the DBP were to remain blinded in the DBP (regardless of whether they had started receiving alternative MS medication). These patients were to continue to be followed up for both efficacy and safety, in line with the Schedule of Activities until the end of the DBP.

Fewer patients discontinued from the study treatment in the OCR arm compared to the FINGO arm. 4 patients in the OCR arm discontinued from treatment, none due to AE or lack of efficacy. In the FINGO arm 13 patients discontinued, 3 patients due to AEs and 4 due to lack of efficacy, 2 discontinued prior to receiving any treatment.

All 15 study patients who discontinued from the study treatment after receiving at least one dose of study drug (4 in the OCR arm and 11 in the FINGO arm) withdrew from the study and did not continue with their efficacy and safety follow-up. Numerically the median time from first study drug administration to discontinuation was longer in the ocrelizumab treated group (687.5 days [IQR range 395-815.5] versus 365 days [IQR range 76-486 days]).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

One patient in the OCR arm and 4 patients in the FINGO arm were discontinued from the study and unblinded prematurely in accordance with protocol instructions.

## Recruitment

First patient enrolled: 19 th of May 2022

Last patient enrolled: 7 th of November 2024

Data cut-off: 9 th June 2025

Final CSR report date: August 2025

## Conduct of the study

## Protocol amendments

The first version of the protocol is dated on 30th of July 2020. At time of first randomization protocol version 3 was in effect (26th April 2022). At time of submission the newest version was protocol version 6 released May 6th 2024. The most relevant changes in study conduct/protocol amendments, after first randomization, are reviewed below

## Protocol version 4 (28th March 2023)

- Study was amended to open enrolment of patients with a BW &gt;40 kg.
- Duration was updated to a flexible duration of at least 24 weeks from the last patient randomized.
- Secondary efficacy objectives were updated to remove the endpoints of number of new or enlarging T2 lesions by Week 96 and protocol-defined ARR by Week 96.
- Sample size, non-inferiority margin, and power were updated.
- The inclusion criterion requesting for neurologic stability for ≥30 days prior to screening and between screening and baseline was removed to align with current clinical practice. This criterion was no longer required to avoid delays in treatment start so that patients can benefit early from an effective MS therapy, and in the absence of any safety concerns.
- The exclusion criterion regarding the time elapsed since treatment with systemic corticosteroid therapy was updated to 7 days prior to treatment allocation, to allow for patients who may experience a MS relapse during the screening period to receive adequate corticosteroid treatment, without impacting their safety or the PD effects of ocrelizumab.

## Protocol amendment 5 (27 September 2023)

The Protocol was amended for the purposes of transitioning to the Clinical Trials Regulation in the European Union. This was an administrative protocol only, and was not implemented at any study sites.

## Protocol amendment 6 (6 May 2024)

- Inclusion criteria has been updated to include patients with BW ≤ 40 kg to align with the Internal Monitoring Committee's recommendations from Study WA39085 (phase 2 study with ocrelizumab in pediatric patients with MS). Dosing regimen for ocrelizumab and fingolimod depending on patients' BW have been updated accordingly.
- The wash-out period for immunosuppressants has been updated to reflect the biological half-life of these medications.

## Changes to the SAP

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The first SAP was dated on the 9 th November 2024. The second SAP version was dated on 29 August 2025.

The key changes to the SAP, along with the rationale(s) for each change, are presented in Table 11.

| 2.1       | Justification of non-inferiority margin has been added.                                                                                                          | For clarity                                                                                                 |
| 4.2.3     | The condition of more than 5% of patients in ocrelizumab arm withdraw from treatment has been removed. The methodology of tipping point analysis has been added. | The sensitivity will be performed regardless of the rates of treatment discontinuation as suggested by FDA. |

| 4.5.1.3   | Treatment duration of ocrelizumab/placebo and fingolimod/placebo have been updated. Both end dates have been updated to treatment withdrawal or CCOD.            | For consistency                                                                                             |

## Protocol deviations

MPD were defined as potentially impacting study integrity or patient safety and wellbeing. These were classified into four main categories: procedural, medication, inclusion criteria, and exclusion criteria; and an assessment of their impact was undertaken by the Sponsor.

MPD were reported in 134 of 187 patients randomized in the study: 67.7% (63 patients) in the OCR arm and 75.5% (71 patients) in the FINGO arm. The most common category of MPD was procedural, specifically: violations of the informed consent process (17.2% [16 patients] in the OCR arm and 29.8% [28 patients] in the FINGO arm, these occurrences being mainly related to local regulations not being followed), EDSS assessments not performed as per protocol (18.3% [17 patients] in the OCR arm and 20.2% [19 patients] in the FINGO arm), and failure to maintain blinding procedures (9.7% [9 patients] in the OCR arm and 18.1% [17 patients] in the FINGO arm). Failure to perform EDSS strictly per protocol was mainly due to late or missing EDSS assessment, but it occurred infrequently in patients with relapse, and for those cases relapse confirmation was still ensured through EDSS/FS increases; therefore, there was no impact on the primary analysis. Failure to maintain blinding procedures was mainly due to isolated procedural deviations (e.g., related to site personnel accessing Day 1 ECG data, MRI reports, or blinded laboratory values), none of which led to actual treatment unblinding, and hence there was no impact on the primary analysis.

For frequency counts by deviation, multiple occurrences of the same deviation in an individual were counted only once; therefore, the total number of 497 deviations included multiple deviations reported in single patients. There are patients with repeated occurrences. These were related to the following subcategories: failure to perform telephone interview within 24 hours after infusion or any interview between study visits, incomplete telephone interviews as per protocol, violations of the informed consent process - procedural, or EDSS assessments not performed as per protocol.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

There were no MPD related to the COVID-19 pandemic. Thirteen major protocol deviations were recorded for patients incorrectly stratified at randomization due to inaccurate pre-pubertal status data reported by the site in Interactive X Response System. The correct pre-pubertal status, based on Tanner Stage, was accurately captured in the eCRF and used for the primary analyses. This stratification error did not impact study results, since the overall study population was predominantly pubertal, with 3 patients being pre-pubertal.

None of the patients with protocol deviations were excluded from the study. Overall, the number and types of MPD were balanced between the two study arms. Following a thorough review of each major deviation, including root cause analysis and impact assessment, the Sponsor has determined that there was no impact on the interpretation of the primary analysis of the study or the safe participation in the study.

## Baseline data

At baseline, demographic characteristics such as age, sex, race, ethnicity, weight, and height were well balanced between the OCR and FINGO arms (Table 12). The majority of patients were female at 69% (67 and 62 patients in the OCR and FINGO arms, respectively), and predominantly White (65.8% [123 patients]) and Not Hispanic or Latino (56.1% [105 patients]) (Table 12). It should also be noted that for some patients, data on ethnicity (19.3%) and race (18.2%) was \"Not reported\" due to local regulations in several countries prohibiting the collection of this information (e.g., France, Austria). The median weight and height at baseline were 63.1 kg (range: 42.3-154.2 kg) and 166.4 cm (range: 144.5-189.0 cm), respectively. The median body mass index was 23.1 kg/m2 (range: 16.4-59.5). The median age of patients was 15 years (range: 11-17 years). A total of 33 patients were less than 14 years of age. Due to the low prevalence of pre-pubertal patients (defined as Tanner stage &lt; 2), only 3 pre-pubertal patients were randomized, 1 patient in the OCR arm and 2 patients in the FINGO arm.

Overall, baseline MS disease characteristics were comparable between the OCR and FINGO arms (Table 12). The median EDSS score was 1.5 in both the OCR and FINGO arms (range: 0.0-4.5 and 0.0-5.5, respectively) (Table 12). The number of relapses in the last 1 year and in the last 2 years were well balanced, with the mean (standard deviation [SD]) number of relapses in all patients being 1.4 (0.8) in the last 1 year and 1.8 (1.0) in the last 2 years (Table 12). Overall, 47.1% (88 patients) presented with T1 Gd-enhanced lesions at baseline (Table 12). The number of T1 Gd-enhancing lesions and T2 lesions at baseline were also comparable between the two arms, with the mean (SD) number of Gdenhancing T1 lesions at baseline of 2.5 (4.8) and 2.3 (4.5), and the mean (SD) number of hyperintense T2 lesions at baseline of 57.4 (46.6) and 57.4 (48.2) in the OCR and FINGO arms, respectively. There were fewer patients in the OCR arm than the FINGO arm who received prior treatment with any DMTs prior to baseline (14% [13 patients] in the OCR arm and 22.3% [21 patients] in the FINGO arm). The mean (SD) duration of disease in terms of time from MS symptoms onset was 1.1 (1.1) years in the OCR arm and 1.3 (1.2) years in the FINGO arm (Table 12).

(a) MRI data from one randomized patient, who did not receive study treatment, was not transferred from NeuroRx. Percentages are calculated based on the number of patients in the treatment arm (N). n represents the number of patients contributing to summary statistics.

There were no clinically relevant imbalances in prior medical conditions between patients in OCR arm and patients in FINGO arm. 37.6% (35 patients) in the OCR arm and 39.4% (37 patients) in the FINGO arm had at least one previous medical history.

The most common previous medical condition by System Organ Class (SOC) (≥5% of patients in either arm [OCR vs. FINGO]) were:

- Infections and infestations: 17.2% (16 patients) vs. 19.1% (18 patients)
- Nervous system disorders: 6.5% (6 patients) vs. 6.4% (6 patients)
- Injury, poisoning, and procedural complications: 2.2% (2 patients) vs. 6.4% (6 patients)
- Respiratory, thoracic, and mediastinal disorders: 5.4% (5 patients) vs. 3.2% (3 patients)

Concurrent medical history was balanced between the treatment arms. 65.6% (61 patients) in the OCR arm and 67.0% (63 patients) in the FINGO arm had at least one concurrent medical condition.

Overall, 67.0% (124 patients) received at least one prior medication (i.e., medication that started prior to but not ongoing at the time of first dose of study drug). 68.8% (64 patients) in the OCR arm and 65.2% (60 patients) in the FINGO arm received at least one prior medication. Apart from vaccines, the most frequently observed classes of prior medications used (≥10% patients in either arm [OCR vs. FINGO]) were:

- Ophthalmologicals: 25.8% (24 patients) vs. 21.7% (20 patients)
- Corticosteroids, dermatological preparations: 23.7% (22 patients) vs. 19.6% (18 patients)
- Immunostimulants: 10.8% (10 patients) vs. 16.3% (15 patients)

Medication used at any time since the first dose of study drug (excluding IRR premedication) is reported. Overall, 81.6% (151 patients) received at least one concomitant medication in the study. 86.0% (80 patients) in the OCR arm and 77.2% (71 patients) in the FINGO arm received at least one concomitant medication. The most frequently observed classes of concomitant medications used were:

- Analgesics: (60.2% vs. 51.1%), with paracetamol as the most frequent (46.2% vs. 39.1%)
- Anti-inflammatory and antirheumatic products: (48.4% vs. 35.9%), with ibuprofen as the most frequent medication in this class (34.4% vs. 23.9%)
- Ophthalmologicals (55.9% vs. 52.2%)
- Other dermatological preparations (46.2% vs. 34.8%)
- Other gynecologicals (41.9% vs. 32.6%), with ibuprofen (34.4% vs. 23.9%) as the most frequent medication in this class
- Topical products for joint and muscular pain (49.5% vs. 32.6%)

Patients in the OCR arm received more anti-inflammatory and antirheumatic products, topical products for joint and muscular pain, and other gynecologicals than patients in the FINGO arm. Gynecologicals treatments were mainly provided for dysmenorrhea events.

## Numbers analysed

No patients were excluded from efficacy analyses. The full analysis set included 187 subjects (93 OCR and 94 FINGO).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Outcomes and estimation

## 1. Primary efficacy endpoint (non-inferiority)

In the ocrelizumab arm 9 patients had 10 relapses during the trial, 6/10 occurred before 24 weeks (=168 days) with 4 occurring before 12 weeks. In the fingolimod arm 13 patients had 16 relapses, of which 6/16 (37.5%) occurred in the first 24 weeks of the trial and 5/16 (31%) occurred before week 12. In both arms the relapse rate is low compared with baseline relapse rates of about 1.5 per year. The study met its primary endpoint: during the DBP, the adjusted protocol-defined ARR was 0.070 in patients treated with ocrelizumab and 0.135 in patients treated with fingolimod, with a rate ratio of 0.517 (95% CI: 0.193, 1.329). The upper bound of the 95% CI fell below the predefined noninferiority margin of 3, demonstrating that ocrelizumab is non-inferior to fingolimod in reducing ARR.

## Sensitivity analysis

A tipping point sensitivity analysis was performed to assess the robustness of the estimated treatment effect to the proposed missing data handling strategy. Even with a penalty of 10, ocrelizumab is still non-inferior to fingolimod in reducing ARR, further strengthening confidence in the study's primary conclusions.

## Subgroup analyses:

The effect on ARR in subgroups was consistent with that observed in the overall population (Figure 20), demonstrating the consistency of the treatment effect with ocrelizumab across the subgroups. The small sample size in certain subgroups should be considered when interpreting these results. Subgroup analysis of baseline weight &lt; 35 kg could not be performed due to absence of randomized patients weighing less than 35 kg.

The rate ratio, confidence intervals and dispersion parameter are estimated from a Negative Binomial regression model for the total number of events adjusted for the following covariates:(region [U.S. vs. RoW], pre-pubertal status (yes vs. no), and presence of T1 Gd lesions at baseline [yes vs. no]). For treatment comparisons, the reference is Treatment Fingolimod. The pre-pubertal status may be removed due to model convergence issues. The analysis uses the hypothetical estimand, assuming a hypothetical situation in which patients neither withdraw from the study treatment nor take any other MS disease-modifying therapies (DMT). Exact Poisson test is used to if total count is zero.

## Supplementary analyses to the primary endpoint:

The supplementary estimand using treatment policy for the intercurrent event of treatment discontinuation was not provided because not enough patients discontinued to trigger the supplemental analysis according to the SAP.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The supplementary analysis using the primary estimand adjusted for pre-specified stratification factors and neurological stability for at least 30 days yielded to an ARR ratio 0.514 95% CI (0.191, 1.322). The supplementary analysis using the primary estimand excluding patients who did not meet the exclusion criteria or did not meet the eligibility criteria at baseline yielded to an ARR ratio 0.517 95% CI (0.193, 1.329).

## 2. Secondary efficacy endpoints

## Number of new or enlarging T2 lesions (non-inferiority and superiority)

During the DBP most patients had new or enlarging T2 lesions (75.5% in FINGO, 78.5% in OCR) compared to baseline.

The study met its first secondary endpoint as the upper bound of the 95% CI [0.359, 0.760] fell below the predefined non-inferiority margin of 1.27, demonstrating that ocrelizumab is non-inferior to fingolimod in reducing the appearance of new or enlarging T2 lesions (non-inferiority).

Furthermore, the annualized rate of the number of new or newly enlarged T2 lesions was statistically significantly lower in paediatric patients treated with ocrelizumab (rate per year 3.827 [95% CI 2.931, 4.996] in OCR versus 7.275 [95% CI 5.606, 9.442] in FINGO), with a rate ratio of 0.522 (95% CI: 0.359, 0.760; p = 0.001), compared with fingolimod. Therefore, the study met its second secondary endpoint. Treatment with ocrelizumab resulted in a 47.8% reduction in the number of new or newly enlarged T2 lesions compared with fingolimod. Effect is consistent across subgroups (Figure 21).

Figure 21: Annualized Rate of New or Enlarging T2 lesions during the DBP by Subgroup

<!-- image -->

The rate ratio, confidence intervals and dispersion parameter are estimated from a Negative Binomial regression model for the total number of events adjusted for the following covariates:(region [U.S. vs. RoW], pre-pubertal status (yes vs. no), and presence of T1 Gd lesions at baseline [yes vs. no]). For treatment comparisons, the reference is Treatment Fingolimod. The pre-pubertal status may be removed due to model convergence issues. The analysis uses the hypothetical estimand, assuming a hypothetical situation in which patients neither withdraw from the study treatment nor take any other MS disease-modifying therapies (DMT). Exact Poisson test is used for small sample sizes.

## Number of Gd-enhancing T1 lesions (superiority)

At Week 12, the number of Gd-enhancing T1 lesions was statistically significantly lower in pediatric patients treated with ocrelizumab (rate per scan 0.054 [95%CI 0.017, 0.125]), with a rate ratio of 0.128 (95% CI: 0.034, 0.414; p = 0.001), compared with fingolimod (rate per scan 0.375 [95%CI 0.258, 0.527]). Treatment with ocrelizumab resulted in an 87.2% reduction in the number of Gdenhancing T1 lesions compared with fingolimod. Thus, the study meets the third secondary endpoint.

## Annualized relapse rate (superiority)

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

OCR was not superior to FINGO in reduction of ARR (adjusted rate ratio 0.517 [95% CI: 0.193, 1.329; p=0.159]). The study did not meet the fourth secondary endpoint.

This AR only presents the most relevant exploratory endpoints for the assessment

## Time to first relapse

The median time to first event was not reached in either treatment arm due to the low number of events during the DBP (Figure 22).

## Confirmed disability progression (12- and 24 weeks)

The analyses reported in the OPERETTA 2 clinical study report utilized a \"discontinuation-as-event\" imputation rule. Following concerns raised about high 12- and 24 week CDP rates in OPERETTA 2, the MAH recalculated the CDP rates by applying two additional handling methods for the intercurrent event of treatment withdrawal:

1. Censoring: Patients who withdraw from treatment are treated as censored at the time of discontinuation.
2. Hybrid: Treatment withdrawal due to \"lack of efficacy\" are imputed as event, whereas those due to other reasons are censored at the time of discontinuation.

Furthermore, to allow for a comparison with the CDP event rate observed in the fingolimod Phase III trial in paediatric patients CFTY720D2311 (PARADIGMS), the MAH has also performed this analysis using the CDP definition from the PARADIGMS publication (Deiva et al, 2020). Critically, in PARADIGMS, an EDSS worsening of at least 1.5 points was required for patients with a baseline EDSS = 0, whereas in OPERETTA 2, a 1-point change was applied to baseline EDSS scores of 0. This CDP definition is particularly relevant in paediatric MS studies, given that many patients have a baseline EDSS=0 (in OPERETTA 2, 27% [50/185] of patients had an EDSS = 0)).

Table 14 shows the results as of the primary CSR CCOD of 09 Jun 2025 when using different imputation rules (original, censoring and hybrid) and when using different CDP definition.

## Patient and caregiver report quality of life

For the patient-reported PedsQL, median (range) total scores at baseline were similar for the OCR and FINGO arms (72.83 [36.96 to 94.57] and 71.74 [34.78 to 100.00], respectively). The median (range) change in PedsQL total score from baseline to Weeks 48 and 96 was 7.61 (-32.61 to 32.61) and 8.70 (-29.35 to 30.43), respectively, for patients in the OCR arm and was 3.26 (-25.00 to 26.09) and 3.26 (-18.48 to 17.39), respectively, for patients in the FINGO arm. A higher score indicated better health related quality of life (0-100).

For the caregiver-reported PedsQL, median (range) total scores at baseline were similar for the OCR and FINGO arms (71.74 [22.83 to 98.91] and 72.28 [33.70 to 98.91], respectively). The median (range) change in PedsQL total score from baseline to Weeks 48 and 96 was 4.89 (-38.04 to 36.96) and 1.09 (-23.91 to 21.74), respectively, as reported by caregivers for patients in the OCR arm and 1.09 (-6.52 to 38.04) and 2.72 (15.22 to 40.22), respectively, as reported by caregivers for patients in the FINGO arm. A higher score indicated better health-related quality of life.

For the PedsQL Multidimensional Fatigue scale, the median (range) total scores at baseline were similar for the OCR and FINGO arms (62.50 [15.28 to 95.83] and 66.67 [20.83 to 100.00], respectively). The median (range) change in PedsQL Multidimensional Fatigue total score from baseline to Weeks 48 and 96 was 2.78 (-38.89 to 73.61) and 2.78 (-34.72 to 34.72), respectively, for patients in the OCR arm and was 0.00 (-27.78 to 23.61) and 1.39 (-27.78 to 16.67), respectively, for patients in the FINGO arm. A higher score indicated less fatigue.

## Change in the SDMT from baseline to Week 48 and 96

The median (range) SDMT scores at baseline were similar for the OCR and FINGO arms (52.50 [24.00 - 98.00] and 51.50 [29.00 - 99.00], respectively). In both arms, improvements in cognition were observed at subsequent timepoints but results should be interpreted with caution due to the decrease in available data. During the DBP:

- From baseline to Week 48, the median (range) increase in SDMT was 4.00 [- 23.00 - 43.00] in the OCR arm (65/93 patients with available data) and 5.50 [- 42.00 - 23.00] in the FINGO arm (58/94 patients with available data).
- From baseline to Week 96, the median (range) increase in SDMT was 8.50 [- 8.00 - 30.00] in the OCR arm (28/93 patients with available data) and 9.00 [- 19.00 - 28.00] in the FINGO arm (27/94 patients with available data).

## Biomarkers

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Both treatment arms showed a sustained decrease in serum NfL during the trial.

## Immunogenicity

The prevalence of ADAs at baseline was 0.5% (1/183 patients). Of the 90 patients in the OCR arm with post-baseline assessments, 2 patients (2.2%) showed treatment-emergent ADAs to ocrelizumab. The impact of treatment-emergent ADAs on efficacy and safety endpoints could not be determined given the low incidence.

## Ancillary analyses

Not applicable.

## 2.4.2. Supportive studies

## Open label extension data (OPERETTA 2)

To address the uncertainties around long-term sustained efficacy of ocrelizumab in POMS, the MAH is providing updated efficacy results for OPERETTA 2, ocrelizumab arm (OCR), as of the updated clinical cut-off date (CCOD) of 26 Nov 2025. The results are presented alongside the primary analysis results for the OCR arm (Table 16). As of the updated CCOD, 92 patients initially randomized to the OCR arm were treated for at least 48 weeks, and 47 patients initially randomized to the OCR arm were treated for more than 96 weeks (Table 16). No patients randomized to the OCR arm discontinued from the study between the primary Clinical Study Report (CSR) CCOD and the updated CCOD.

As per the protocol, the frequency of EDSS assessments was reduced from every 12 weeks in DBP to every 24 weeks in the OLE. The MRI assessment schedule in the OLE for patients from the OCR arm consists of a scan at the OLE Day 1 visit if not performed during the previous 4 weeks, followed by a scan at OLE Week 24 and yearly assessments thereafter.

Updated efficacy results are provided in Table 16. Between the primary CSR CCOD and the updated CCOD, no relapse was observed and only a single patient had MRI inflammatory lesions.
