## OPERETTA 1

## Study design

An Open-Label Parallel-Group Study to Evaluate Safety, Tolerability, Pharmacokinetics, and Pharmacodynamic Effects of Ocrelizumab in Children and Adolescents with Relapsing-Remitting Multiple Sclerosis. Study design is show in Figure 4 (PK section).

## Study conduct

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The MAH confirmed that the study was conducted in accordance with applicable laws and regulations, including independent ethics committee and/or institutional review board and the Declaration of Helsinki. The study was conducted in accordance with applicable ICH and GCP guidelines. Patient and/or legally authorized representatives signed an informed consent as per above guidelines and regulations.

## Patient disposition and demographics:

Overall, 23 patients (6 patients in Cohort 1 and 17 patients in Cohort 2) were enrolled in the study, all of whom received at least 1 dose of ocrelizumab. All 23 patients completed the 24-week dose exploration period. 2 patients discontinued.

In Cohort 1, the median age of patients was 11 years (range: 10-12 years). The median weight at baseline was 38.5 kg (range: 27.0-39.0 kg). The median EDSS score was 0.75, and the median duration of disease in terms of time from MS symptoms onset was 0.37 years. None of the patients in Cohort 1 received any MS medication prior to the study. The mean (SD) number of relapses in the prior year was 1.2 (0.4) and in the prior 2 years was 1.3 (0.5). The median number of Gd-enhancing T1 lesions at baseline was 1 (range: 0-11), and the median number of hyperintense T2 lesions at baseline was 43.5 (range: 9-55).

In Cohort 2, the median age of patients was 16 years (range: 11-17 years). The median weight at baseline was 62.4 kg (range: 42.1-108.4 kg). The median EDSS score was 1, and the median duration of disease in terms of time from MS symptoms onset was 0.39 years. The majority (76.5%) of patients in Cohort 2 had not received any MS medication prior to the study. The mean (SD) number of relapses in the prior year was 0.9 (0.2) and in the prior 2 years was 1.1 (0.3). The median number of Gdenhancing T1 lesions at baseline was 0 (range: 0-49), and the median number of hyperintense T2 lesions at baseline was 31.0 (48.9; range: 13-225).

## Efficacy (exploratory endpoints only)

## Clinical and protocol-defined relapses:

As of the CCOD and updated CSR, no clinical relapses were observed in OPERETTA 1.

## Change in EDSS Score

The median baseline EDSS score for Cohort 1 and 2 was 0.75 (range: 0.0 - 2.5) and 1.0 (range: 0.0 - 3.5), respectively. There was a numerical decrease in EDSS score from baseline to the CCOD in both treatment cohorts during the study. The mean change (SD) from baseline to Week 24 was - 0.58 (0.74) in Cohort 1 and - 0.15 (0.58) in Cohort 2.

## Change in Total Number of New and/or Enlarging T2

Ocrelizumab treatment resulted in a reduction in the total number of new and/or enlarging T2 hyperintense lesions in both treatment cohorts as compared to the preceding scan.

- In Cohort 1, 4/6 patients presented new and/or enlarging T2 hyperintense lesions at Week 12. None of the patients had new and/or enlarging T2 hyperintense lesion at Week 24 compared with Week 12. There were no new/enlarging T2 hyperintense lesions from Week 24 onward, up to the CCOD for patients with available data. Note that 5 of 6 patients had imaging at Weeks 72 and 96, and thereafter, fewer patients had reached the later planned study visits.
- Cohort 2: At Week 12, 11 patients showed a total of 65 (range: 1-34) new/enlarging T2 hyperintense lesions. At Week 24, 3 of 17 patients had 3 new/enlarging T2 hyperintense lesions, and, at Week 48, 1 of 17 patients had 1 new/enlarging T2 hyperintense lesions. No new/enlarging lesions were observed at Weeks 72 or 96 among the 17 patients. One patient had 1 new lesion at

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Week 144. Thereafter, no new/enlarging T2 hyperintense lesions were reported in any patients with available data. Note that 12 of 17 patients had imaging at Week 192, and thereafter, fewer patients had reached the later planned study visits.

## Total Number of T1 Gd Lesions at week 12

No new T1 Gd lesions were observed at week 12 in both treatment cohorts as detected by brain MRI scan.

## Change in SDMT Score

The mean (SD) baseline SDMT score for Cohort 1 and 2 was 43.00 (7.87) and 54.56 (21.97), respectively. As of the CCOD, there was a numerical increase in the SDMT score from baseline in both treatment cohorts. The mean change (SD) from baseline to Week 24 was 4.83 (8.06) in Cohort 1 and 7.50 (7.60) in Cohort 2.

## OPERA I WA21092 and OPERA II WA21093 (efficacy in adult RMS)

Data presented below are from the completed double-blind, double-dummy treatment period of the two pivotal Phase III studies in adult RMS (Studies WA21092 and WA21093) up to the clinical cutoff dates of 2 April 2015 and 12 May 2015, respectively. The efficacy results as well as the pooled analysis from these pivotal studies show that ocrelizumab suppresses relapses and disease progression (clinical and subclinical disease activity) compared with IFN β-1a 44 µg SC in patients with RMS over the course of 2 years (96 weeks).

Efficacy outcomes were consistent between trials and across the primary and key clinical and imaging secondary endpoints. In comparison with IFN β-1a 44 µg SC, ocrelizumab 600 mg demonstrated:

- Relative reductions of 46% and 47% for the primary endpoint of protocol-defined ARR in Studies WA21092 and in WA21093, respectively (both p &lt; 0.0001)
- A 40% risk reduction for 12-week CDP in the pooled Study WA21092/WA21093 analysis (p=0.0006). Each individual trial also demonstrated a significant reduction of 12-week CDP (43% reduction [p = 0.0139] in Study WA21092 and 37% [p = 0.0169] in Study WA21093).
- Relative reductions of 94% and 95% in the number of T1 Gd lesions in Studies WA21092 and WA21093, respectively (both p &lt; 0.0001)
- Relative reductions of 77% and 83% in the number of new and/or enlarging T2 lesions in Studies WA21092 and WA21093, respectively (both p &lt; 0.0001)
- A 33% relative increase in proportion of patients with 12-week confirmed disability improvement in the pooled Study WA21092/WA21093 analysis (p = 0.0194). Study WA20192 demonstrated a 61% relative increase in the proportion of patients with 12-week confirmed disability improvement (p=0.0106); whereas in Study WA21093, there was no statistically significant difference between treatment groups (14% relative increase, p=0.4019).
- A 40% risk reduction for 24-week CDP in the pooled Study WA21092/WA21093 analysis (p=0.0025). Each individual trial also demonstrated a significant reduction of 24-week CDP (43% reduction [p=0.0278] in Study WA21092 and 37% [p=0.0370] in Study WA21093).
- Relative reductions of 57% and 64% (both p&lt;0.0001) in the number of new T1-hypointense lesions (chronic black holes) in Studies WA21092 and WA21093, respectively

## Real-world evidence

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A study reporting data in 10 patients with pediatric RRMS treated with ocrelizumab showed a notable reduction in ARR and no observed MRI activity during treatment (Amirov et al. 2023). Mean ARR decreased from 2.01 (±0.71) to 0 during ocrelizumab treatment (p &lt; 0.0001; median duration of follow-up: 28.3 months).

A retrospective cohort study including POMS patients who initiated treatment with fingolimod and ocrelizumab before the age of 18 years at 12 regional pediatric MS referral centers showed rapid control of disease activity following treatment initiation with fingolimod and ocrelizumab (Shukla et al. 2023). It included 96 patients on fingolimod and 37 on ocrelizumab with high pre-baseline disease activity. A large majority of patients treated with ocrelizumab (91%) achieved NEDA-2 within the first 6 months, a status defined by the absence of clinical relapses and new MRI activity. This treatment effect was well-maintained, with 89% of patients continuing to show no evidence of disease activity at 12 months. While exact relapse rates for the initial 6 months were not provided, NEDA-2 serves as a comprehensive measure reflecting both clinical and radiological disease activity. From this cohort, a subsequent analysis was conducted on a subset of patients who had MRI and/or clinical relapse followup available more than 3 months after treatment began and includes efficacy data from 87 patients on fingolimod and 52 patients on ocrelizumab (Nasr et al. 2024). Fingolimod reduced the relapse rate from 0.43 to 0.12 and new T2 lesions from 78% to 47%, while ocrelizumab decreased the relapse rate from 0.64 to 0.09 with no new T2 lesions observed during treatment. The median follow-up times were 22 months for fingolimod and 15 months for ocrelizumab.

A retrospective study in 24 POMS patients reported that 91.7% of patients were relapse-free after ocrelizumab treatment (median duration of follow-up: 15 months), with a decrease in the mean ARR from 1.08 to 0.08 (p &lt; 0.001) (Alroughani et al. 2025).

Another retrospective cohort study in pediatric patients with RRMS (14 on fingolimod and 22 on ocrelizumab) reported similar results, with both treatments reducing relapse rate (ARR from 1.4 (± 1.7) pre-ocrelizumab to 0 during treatment [p=0.0004]) and new T2 lesions (Spirek and Brenton 2025).

A most recent publication (Altelly et al. 2025) on a retrospective cohort of 29 POMS patients treated with ocrelizumab showed that the mean ARR decreased from 5.42 ± 6.02 to 0.023 ± 0.12 during ocrelizumab treatment (p &lt; 0.0001; median duration of follow-up:14 months (6-19 months). EDSS scores remained stable.

## 2.4.3. Discussion on clinical efficacy

## Design and conduct of clinical studies

This Type II variation application seeks approval for a new indication for the approved medicinal product Ocrevus (ocrelizumab) 300 mg concentrate for solution for infusion (intravenous use) for paediatric patients aged 10 years and older with relapsing-remitting multiple sclerosis (RRMS).

OPERETTA 2 (study WN42096) was a Phase III multi-center, randomized, double-blind, double dummy, parallel-group, non-inferiority study in children and adolescents with RMS. The active comparator was fingolimod, which is approved in the EU for treatment RMS in children and adolescents aged 10-17 years old with highly active disease and/or treatment failure according to pre-specified criteria. Fingolimod was approved based on PARADIGM, a randomized, double-blind, superiority study (active comparator interferon beta 1a) in a similar paediatric population with RMS. The ocrelizumab adult RMS indication was approved in the EU in 2018 based on OPERA 1 and 2, which were randomized, double-blind superiority studies in RMS against the same active comparator, interferon beta.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In OPERETTA 2 study duration was flexible, i.e. until the last randomized patient had completed a minimum of 24 weeks in the DBP. Use of a flexible duration study has been discussed several times with the PDCO as the MAH proposed this design in PIP modification requests. In particular, it has been questioned by the PDCO if 24 weeks is sufficient to demonstrate efficacy in RMS, including whether onset of efficacy for fingolimod is expected at 24 weeks, because a non-inferiority study implies that sufficient efficacy of the comparator is reached at time of analysis. The MAH provided several lines of evidence (to the PDCO) that fingolimod efficacy can be assessed at 24 weeks. In adults, fingolimod reduced the ARR compared to placebo at 3 months (0.32 versus 0.52, p=0.0015) and 6 months (0.21 versus 0.45, p&lt;0.0001) (Kappos et al., 2016, PMID: 26645392). A tipping point analysis, based on derived ARRs from PARADIGM, support that this is also the case in the paediatric population. PDCO also raised concerns about setting a precedent, deviating from the standard 96-week trial observation period typically requested by the PDCO (and as used in most adult clinical trials). The PARADIGM study had a similar flexible study duration (mean treatment duration in PARADIGM 80.3 weeks [SD 24.8], mean treatment duration in OPERETTA 2 78.5 [SD 36.2]). It was acknowledged by the PDCO that an unmet need exists, and that off-label use ocrelizumab is already used in the paediatric population with RMS. As the CHMP has previously accepted the PARADIGM study, and has also endorsed the shared biology of RMS in children and adults (Tecfidera EPAR), it was agreed by the PDCO that the flexible study duration could be acceptable on certain conditions. The MAH was requested to increase the sample size, provide real world data, ensure inclusion of at least 145 patients at week 48 and 71 patients at week 96 to mitigate the risk of falsely concluding non-inferiority, double the number of patients aged &lt;14 years old to n=30, and add secondary endpoints (analysis of ARR and N/E T2 lesions at week 48 and 96). The CHMP position is that the flexible duration can be endorsed, in this specific situation, and therefore supports the PDCO conclusion. It is noted that the MAH is in full compliance with the PIP, including the listed conditions. Nevertheless, the long-term sustainability of ocrelizumab efficacy in POMS is identified as an uncertainty by way of the trial design. To some extent, the same uncertainty also applies to adult RMS trials due to the nature of the condition.

The planned clinical follow-up and schedule of activities of patients during the DBP of OPERETTA 2 included onsite visits every 12 weeks, of which every other visit was a treatment visit (ocrelizumab or ocrelizumab placebo). This was supplemented with telephone interviews and a re-treatment assessment 2 weeks prior to dosing. MRI was done every 24 weeks during the trial (without gadolinium) and at week 12 (with gadolinium) which is endorsed. The planned visits and patient follow-up were considered sufficient to assess the proposed endpoints of the study. Safe guards against functional unblinding were used as relevant (blinded assessment of MRI and specific laboratory analyses, use of examining and treating investigator, as well as a separate investigator for 1 st time fingolimod treatment, because of the potentially revealing bradycardic effect of fingolimod). Cases of functional unblinding of the treating investigator are likely due to protocol-mandated unblinding of specific critical laboratory tests and MRI results for safety reasons. Mainly, unblinding of hematologic tests (lymphocytopenia, leukopenia) in about 16 patients in the fingolimod arm may have caused functional unblinding of the treating investigator due to fingolimod's known mechanism of action (sequestration of lymphocytes removing them from peripheral circulation). However, considering the use of examining physician, who remained blinded, and the low number per trial site, it is expected that the risk of this adversely affecting data integrity is minimal.

The dosing recommendation in the SmPC for patients weighing&lt;35 kgs cannot be evaluated based on OPERETTA 2 data alone as all included patients fulfilled criteria for use of adult doses (weight &gt;35 kg). In OPERETTA 1, 6 patients had a BW &lt;40 kg, of these 2 weighed &lt;35 kg. In OPERETTA 2, no patients weighed &lt;50 kg. From OPERA 1 and 2 there is adult PK data in the weight range 40-50 kg (estimated &gt; 30 data points). No PK data of 600 mg dosing of patients weighing ≥35 and &lt;40 kg were collected, as the cut-off for the adult 600 mg dose in OPERETTA 1 was 40 kg Predicted exposures demonstrated exposure within the observed adult range for patients treated with both 300 and 600 mg (given a 35 kg cut-off for a 300 mg dose). Since no patients between 35 and 40 kg were dosed with 600 mg (only modelled data are available) and very few were below 35 kg, information on this uncertainty has been included in section 4.2 and 5,2 of the SmPC.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The MAH chose a non-inferiority design, and while this is not ideal, especially in a small population, it is in line with the PIP and can be accepted as per the EMA guideline on MS. Important considerations in case of non-inferiority trials is control of assay sensitivity, use of a reasonable non-inferiority margin, and use of an active comparator, which is not a first line DMT (Guideline on clinical investigation of medicinal products for the treatment of multiple sclerosis, EMA/CHMP/771815/2011 revision 2, 2015).

It is noted that the active comparator, fingolimod, is not generally considered a first line DMT and/or DMT with low-moderate efficacy although final consensus in the area of DMT classification is not reached (SmPC recommends use only in highly active disease, Freeman et al., 2022, CNS Drugs). Given that fingolimod has shown level 1A evidence of efficacy in POMS the choice of fingolimod as active comparator is considered to reduce concerns about assay sensitivity (\"biocreep\", controls assay sensitivity). In terms of the non-inferiority margin, for the primary endpoint this is 3 (ARR ratio, upper bound of 95% CI), based on PARADIGM, according to the MAH. This margin implies that an annualized relapse rate up to three times higher in the ocrelizumab arm than in the fingolimod arm is allowed, which appears difficult to justify clinically However, this concern had not an impact on the study objectives in the view of the results from secondary endpoints showing superiority vs. fingolimod.

Finally, the pivotal ocrelizumab adult studies (OPERA 1 and 2) provide clear, level 1A evidence of efficacy in adult RMS. Ocrelizumab has been marketed in the EU since 2018 for adult onset RMS and its efficacy is well-established in adults with RMS, including as a HE-DMT. The CHMP has previously recognized the shared biology of RMS in children and adults, including a comparable natural history and clinical features (Tecfidera EPAR). These circumstances, and the choice of a HE-DMT as comparator, are considered by the CHMP to reduce concerns about a false conclusion of non-inferiority.

In- and exclusion criteria were found relevant and acceptable. It is noted that they ensure appropriate wash-out for previous MS treatments (or prohibit them if this is not possible), and safe-guard - to the extent possible - against misdiagnosis of POMS. The use of the 2017 McDonald criteria (Thompson et al., 2018, Lancet Neurology) to confirm RMS in children is supported considering the timepoint for trial initiation. It is noted that these criteria were mainly developed based on data from populations, in which the a priori likelihood of MS, is much higher than in children and/or adolescents. Although their use across both AOMS (adult onset multiple sclerosis) and POMS is well accepted, the McDonald criteria do require exclusion of other more likely explanations, i.e. they are not designed to rule out other conditions in and of themselves. Therefore, use of these criteria alone is not a sufficient safe guard against wrongful diagnosis, especially not in paediatric patients, in which the a priori likelihood of RMS is low (Thompson et al., Lancet Neurology, 2018, \"Diagnosis of multiple sclerosis: 2017 revision of the McDonald criteria). A new set of diagnostic criteria was published recently (Montalban et al., 2025, Lancet Neurology). It is the assessment of the CHMP that the use of an independent review committee to verify the diagnosis in OPERETTA 2, combined with relevant in- and exclusion criteria (i.e. with respect to MOG antibodies and ADEM), account for the risk of misdiagnosis in the paediatric population, and that they nicely align the population with the updated McDonald criteria published in 2025, even if these criteria were not formally used (i.e. MOG antibody testing). The in- and exclusion criteria do not mandate CSF testing or spinal cord MRI at the time of screening although these remain relevant for differential diagnosis in populations with a low a priori likelihood of RMS (i.e. the pediatric population). As information about previous spinal cord MRI and CSF analysis results could be made available to the independent central reviewer (in the Patient Diagnosis Form or by request), who confirmed the RMS diagnosis before randomization, exclusion of these procedures at screening is acceptable. The overall assessment of the CHMP is that the OPERETTA 2 trial population is representative of the general POMS population, which supports the external validity of the trial. The primary endpoint was ARR (flexible duration, minimum 24 weeks in DBP, as described above). The comparison of interest is the difference of protocol-defined ARR, as measured through the rate ratio in counts during the DBP. This is acceptable and in line with current international trial standards and the PIP. The definition and assessment of protocol-defined relapse is considered acceptable and in line with consensus publications (Thompson et al., 2018) and clinical trials in the same population i.e. PARADIGM (Chitnes et al., 2018). The primary endpoint cannot assess long-term disability progression in this population, and a reduction of ARR over a span of 24-96 weeks cannot be taken as a marker of decreased risk of future, long-term disability (Guideline on clinical investigation of medicinal products for the treatment of multiple sclerosis, EMA/CHMP/771815/2011 revision 2, 2015). Development of long-term disability could be considered the most important outcome of interest, although it is acknowledged that investigation hereof, in a controlled clinical trial set-up in POMS, is not feasible, considering that such disability typically occurs &gt;5 years (or even decades) after POMS diagnosis. Given the trial design and the difficult-to-study, rare population some uncertainty regarding long-term disability is unavoidable (as is also the case in adults), and does not in itself prevent the benefit/risk from being positive in RMS (adult and paediatric). As detailed in the results section, the MAH has provided further evidence to reduce this uncertainty. New or enlarging T2 lesions are an appropriate measure for detection of radiological disease activity and for assessment of treatment effectiveness The proposed secondary endpoints are appropriate, and include number of new or enlarging T2hyperintense lesions as detected by brain MRI (non-inferiority and superiority) and number of T1 Gd enhancing lesions (superiority) at week 12. It is noted that the MAH also included the secondary endpoints ARR and new or enlarging T2 hyperintense lesions at week 48 and 96 (referred to as \"EEA specific\" endpoints), as requested by the PDCO. A biomarker analysis of NfL chain levels in serum was also included. NfL is a general marker of axonal damage, and is therefore of interest in POMS. The MAH specified estimands for all objectives. According to the pre-specified estimand, the intercurrent events of treatment discontinuation and initation of another DMD should have been handled using hypothetical strategy. However, it is then stated that only data collected before the intercurrent event was to be included without any imputation. Therefore, the primary estimand factually employed \"while on treatment\" strategy. For the treatment discontinuation - unless it also entails initiation of another DMD- treatment policy would have been preferable. Nevertheless, it is noted that the MAH included in the SAP a supplementary estimand using treatment policy as strategy for the intercurrent event of treatment discontinuation.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The MAH also performed two supplementary analyses for the primary efficacy endpoint one adjusting for neurological stability (yes/no), defined as patients not experiencing relapse between 30 days prior to screening and the date of randomization and the other excluding patients who did not meet the exclusion criteria or did not meet the eligibility criteria at baseline. To assess the robustness of the estimated treatment effect to the proposed missing data handling strategy for missing data due to early treatment discontinuation, a tipping point analysis was conducted. This is endorsed.

The statistical method was negative binomial model and included log-transformed exposure for each patient as an offset to estimate annualized rates of relapses. (Except for the endpoint of T1 Gd lesions at Week 12, which was modelled without offset). The model is agreed: the negative binomial model is adequate when individual variance is presumably larger than the mean. It is noted that the assigned treatment and the randomization stratification factors were included as independent variables, which is endorsed. Multiplicity control was maintained at an overall 5% level using a gatekeeping strategy using hierarchical ordering. I.e. if a hypothesis was not rejected, testing would stop. This is endorsed.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

After randomization of the first patient the study protocol was amended 3 times (version 4, 5 and 6). Key amendments included changes in BW and dosing, due to results of OPERETTA 1, which was acceptable. Furthermore, changes were made for the inclusion criteria with regard to the use of corticosteroids for relapse treatment prior to randomization (reduced to &gt;7 days). This can be accepted, due to the relatively short half-life of most corticosteroids used for MS relapse.

An extended list of MPD was provided by the MAH. It is clear that MPD occurred across sites, and that several often occurred in the same patient. The MAH position is that the MPDs were not considered to meaningfully compromise the integrity of the trial or interpretability. After evaluation of the MPD, this can be agreed.

## Efficacy data and additional analyses

254 patients were screened and 66 failed screening. 187 patients were randomized to ocrelizumab/fingolimod placebo (n=93) and fingolimod/ocrelizumab placebo (n=94). In the fingolimod arm 2 randomized patients never received the study drug (following randomization 1 patient withdrew, 1 patient was found to violate eligibility prior to dosing). The screen failure rate is low-moderate (screen failure in ~26% of patients). Upon request, the MAH provided a summarized, tabulated overview clearly describing the reason/s for screen failure. Screen failures were widely distributed among the different in- and exclusion criteria; e.g. general health, lab findings, medications, MS criteria etc. It was agreed that reasons do not impact the clinical trial's external validity. Trial sites generally enrolled a low number of patients (2-10) consistent with the rarity of POMS. The proportion of participants enrolled in the EU was considered sufficient (53.5%) considering the epidemiology of POMS.

Discontinuation during the DBP occurred in 4 patients (4.3%) in the ocrelizumab arm and 13 patients (13.8%) in the fingolimod arm (total n=17, 9.1%), thus discontinuations were skewed in favour of ocrelizumab. The protocol emphasizes that participants, who withdraw from study treatment, are encouraged to stay in the trial, nevertheless, all 15 dosed patients also discontinued from the trial. This pattern was likely due to the pre-defined option of being informed about treatment allocation in the event of trial discontinuation. It is acknowledged that there would be significant ethical concerns related to unintentionally repeating a treatment (i.e. open label fingolimod) for highly inflammatory POMS if this treatment had been inefficacious or not tolerated during the DBP of OPERETTA 2. This would be a clinical dilemma as fingolimod, the active comparator, is available to patients with POMS in EU, as the only other highly efficacious therapy indicated for POMS. Such code-break was done at least 5 times, potentially up to 15. Although these code breaks are not ideal, the pre-defined nature and the fact that measures were taken to avoid dissemination (particularly to the sponsor, examining investigators, MRI readers, etc.) is expected to reduce the risk of bias. While it is difficult to assess the success with which this was accomplished, it is considered sufficient mitigation given the circumstances of the trial. In the ocrelizumab arm 3 of 4 patients who discontinued treatment, discontinued due to \"withdrawal by subject\" and 1 due to \"Other\". In the fingolimod arm discontinuations were mainly due to lack of efficacy (n=4), adverse event (n=3) and \"withdrawal by subject\". Duration of DBP before discontinuation varied, but most patients discontinued after the minimum 24-week DBP (11/15 patients).

Demographic characteristics were sufficiently similar between the two treatment arms at baseline. 33 individuals aged &lt;14 were recruited, which is in line with recommendations from the PDCO. 3 prepubertal patients were recruited in OPERETTA 2 (2 in fingolimod arm, 1 in ocrelizumab arm) and 2 in OPERETTA 1 (ocrelizumab only), which is consistent with the rarity of pre-pubertal POMS. The PIP did not require a specific number of individuals aged 10-12 and/or pre-pubertal. OPERETTA 2 included one 11-year old in the ocrelizumab arm, 12 patients aged 12 years old (7 in ocrelizumab arm) and 20 patients aged 13 years old (6 in ocrelizumab arm). The mean age of 15.0 (SD 1.5) in both arms and the majority of white (63.8-67.7%) females (66-72%) is consistent with the patient population most at risk for both POMS and AOMS. With reference to the previous discussion on weight-based dosing in patients weighing &lt;35 kg (please refer to section of clinical pharmacology) it is noted that the mean weight in OPERETTA 2 was 68.3 (SD17.8) and the median weight was 63.1 (range 42.3-154.2). The MAH proposes a therapeutic indication down to 10 years of age, in line with the ocrelizumab PIP, which is fulfilled. AOMS and POMS are considered to share a common pathophysiology, although in prepubertal patients and/or patients below 10-11 years of age the distribution between male and female patients is closer to 50/50 than in adults, where female gender is predominant. In the PIP it is noted that extrapolation of efficacy data is possible based on shared disease pathophysiology. Since 2010 (initial PIP) this understanding has been strengthened. Published guidelines and consensus statements agree that a single diagnostic framework should be applied to AOMS and POMS (Montalban et al., 2025). The similarity between POMS and AOMS has also been recognized by the CHMP in paediatric extensions of indication (i.e. EPAR Tecfidera).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Disease characteristics are sufficiently similar between the two treatment arms except for treatment naivety. While the majority of patients are treatment naïve in the overall population (~82%) the distribution of previous treatment failure is skewed with a higher number in the fingolimod arm (22% versus 14%). This would potentially favour ocrelizumab, given that prior treatment failure indicates high disease activity and/or more difficult to treat disease. This is not ideal, although it should be noted that use of ocrelizumab in highly active and/or treatment refractory disease is well recognized in adults, which can likely be extrapolated to a paediatric population with highly active disease. A subgroup analysis was included, and based on this analysis, the issue is not further pursued. Median EDSS is 1.5 at baseline corresponding to no disability, although the range is 0-5.5 (0=normal neurological exam and 5.5= disability which affects daily routines), indicating that some patients in this population do experience substantial, although not necessarily permanent, disability at the time of study enrolment. T1 Gd lesions, signalling active inflammation with compromised integrity of the blood brain barrier, are present in ~47% of patients at baseline. Considering a mean 1.8 (1) relapses in the past 2 years prior to baseline, the OPERETTA 2 cohort is consistent with the description of POMS as a highly active subset of MS (inflammatory) with lower disability.

Participants' previous and concurrent medical history was reviewed, and was mainly consistent with inclusion of a paediatric population with known RMS disregarding a few potential discrepancies in 5 cases, which can be addressed in the review of MPDs, as requested elsewhere. Previous medical treatments, potentially related to MS, were glatiramer acetate, interferons, teriflunomide and dimethyl fumarate as well as corticosteroids and immunoglobulins, all of which were allowed if certain wash-out periods are maintained (n=25 received immunostimulants, n=10 received immunosuppressant and n=1 received immunoglobulin). Short-term systemic corticosteroids were used according to protocol and standard of care in the event of RMS relapses (9 in ocrelizumab arm, 13 in fingolimod arm). Any other use of systemic corticosteroids was reported as a major protocol deviation (10-13 patients in each arm). As these exposures were short-term and balanced between both arms they were considered unlikely to meaningfully impact the primary endpoint analysis.

93 patients received ocrelizumab and 88 received fingolimod for at least 24 weeks. 68 and 65 patients received treatment in the DBP for 48 weeks (~70%), respectively, and 32 and 31 patients (~35%) for 96 weeks, respectively. 27.8% of patients were followed up in the DBP for &lt;1 year. With reference to the previous discussion on the length of the DBP, it is reassuring that data is provided for substantially more than 24 weeks, in the majority of patients, due to the flexible duration design (across both arms a mean of 82.5 weeks [SD 35.4], median of 78.9 weeks [range 30.7-159.7]).

The study met its pre-defined primary endpoint. The ARR ratio was 0.517 (95% CI: 0.193, 1.329). The overall relapse rate after treatment is overall reassuring, in terms of magnitude, compared to baseline.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Considering the low point estimates for ARR, and the narrow 95% CIs in both arms, the analysis of the primary endpoint indicates that ocrelizumab is indeed non-inferior to fingolimod. While the minimum duration of the DBP was 24 weeks it is evident that the average patient was in the study for much longer (about 80 weeks), which is reassuring, and reduces uncertainties raised about the 24-week minimum period of observation. While the methods included a supplementary estimand for the primary efficacy endpoint using treatment policy for treatment discontinuation, this analysis was not provided because not enough patients discontinued to trigger the supplemental analysis according to the SAP. This is acceptable. It is noted that the factual strategy to handle this intercurrent event was \"while on treatment\" and that 11 out of the 15 dosed patients who discontinued both the treatment and the study, they were retained for the minimum 24-week of the DBP. Considering the primary endpoint was assessed at 24 weeks (ARR), the risk that discontinuations of the other 4 subjects (all in FINGO arm) meaningfully influenced the primary efficacy analysis is considered low. The results from the other two supplementary analyses were in line with the primary efficacy one which is reassuring. To impute the unobserved number of relapses following treatment in the ocrelizumab arm (i.e. relapses that would have happened in the ocrelizumab arm had the patient not withdrawn from the study) a tipping point analysis was done. The most conservative estimate of ARR ratio is 0.6 (95% CI 0.23, 1.57). The tipping point analysis does not suggest that statistical inference or non-inferiority is compromised by study discontinuations. The relevant sub-group analyses appear to support the robustness of the overall efficacy observed in the primary endpoint analysis. Uncertainties remain with respect to the youngest age group (i.e. age &lt;14 years), but no firm conclusions should be drawn based on the low number of events (n=6) and patients (n=33). The study met its pre-defined first (non-inferiority) and second (superiority) secondary endpoints based on new or enlarging T2 lesions. At baseline the mean number of T2 lesions in OPERETTA 2 was 57, although with a significant SD of about 47 (similar in both arms), which is consistent with significant disease activity. At first glance, the annualized rate of new or enlarging T2 lesions after treatment is high in both arms (rate per year 3.827 [95% CI 2.931, 4.996] in ocrelizumab versus 7.275 [95% CI 5.606, 9.442] in fingolimod). It is the assessment of the CHMP that the majority of these events are accrued already at 12 weeks. This is relevant, as recommendations for MRI follow-up include having a new baseline scan 3-6 months after initiation of a DMT (for HE-DMTs with quick onset, such as ocrelizumab). Obtaining a new baseline scan after onset of treatment, for subsequent comparison, is recommended to avoid misinterpretation of treatment effect, based on new or enlarging T2 lesions that had in fact developed before therapeutic onset (MAGNIMS-CMSC-NAIMS 2021). The total number of new or enlarging T2 lesions observed in the ocrelizumab arm during the trial is 495 with the majority, n=479, observed by week 12. Thus, 96.8% of all lesions in the ocrelizumab arm are present at only 12 weeks after treatment initiation, before full therapeutic onset is expected (i.e. at what could be considered the \"new baseline\" MRI). On the contrary, in the fingolimod arm 611 lesions was seen in 63 patients at week 12 (67.3% of all lesions).

A sub-group analysis confirms that ocrelizumab is non-inferior to fingolimod in all subgroups except age &lt;14 years old. For age &lt;14 years old ocrelizumab seems to be inferior to fingolimod (rate 1.08 [95% CI 0.46, 2.55]). However, considering the low number of new or enlarging T2 lesions observed after week 12 of the DBP in the ocrelizumab arm (n=9 in the entire cohort in ocrelizumab versus n=261 in fingolimod) no firm conclusions can be drawn due to a low number of patients and events. The pre-defined superiority analyses of the same endpoint (new or enlarging T2 lesions) shows that ocrelizumab is superior to fingolimod in terms of radiological disease control (new or enlarging T2 lesions rate ratio 0.522 [95% CI 0.359, 0.760]). This reduction is clinically meaningful.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In summary, adequate radiological control is achieved for a large majority of patients treated with ocrelizumab in the DBP within 24 weeks. Even though the initial burden of T2 lesions is high in OPERETTA 2, including at week 12, the present data are highly suggestive that ocrelizumab in the paediatric population has similar, high efficacy on T2 lesions as shown in the adult population, although uncertainties remain for the youngest patients. The data are supportive of the overall conclusions drawn in analyses of the primary endpoint (non-inferiority to fingolimod). The number of T1 Gd enhancing lesions at week 12 are supportive of the primary endpoint and number of new or enlarging T2 lesions. The analysis for superiority against fingolimod for ARR was non-confirmatory. The study is likely underpowered to show superiority against fingolimod and/or patients are followed for too short a period. This endpoint marks the point of non-confirmatory evidence, i.e. testing of any further endpoints, placed lower in the statistical testing hierarchy, are not controlled for multiple testing, and results should be considered non-confirmatory. In the analyses of ARR at week 48 and 96 ocrelizumab results are all numerically in favour of ocrelizumab. The majority of patients were free from clinical relapse during the DBP in both arms. It is acknowledged that CDP is defined as an exploratory endpoint, nevertheless, disability progression is an important endpoint in multiple sclerosis and would often be defined as a secondary endpoint. 12 patients (n=12.9%) in the ocrelizumab arm and n=13 patients (14.1%) had a 24-week CDP event during the trial. This is more than the number of relapses in the trial, which can be due to several factors, such as missed relapses or gradual progression independent of relapse activity (PIRA), as described by Kappos et al., JAMA Neurology, 2020. This publication showed that CDP was often independent of relapses, challenging the traditional distinction between relapsing and progressive MS. Results in both arms are similar, thus do not directly question the non-inferiority of ocrelizumab compared to fingolimod in relapse reduction. Furthermore, the MAH clarified that in OPERETTA 2 a less strict definition for 12- and 24 week CDP was used compared to previous similar trials (PARADIGM). Considering the stricter definition used in OPERETTA 2, and further analyses provided by the MAH, it appears that this drives a significant proportion of the disability progression events compared to PARADIGM findings (unknown imputation strategy, 12-week CDP 4.8% in fingolimod arm). Both treatment arms show a sustained decrease in serum NfL during the trial (although in the higher spectrum of the normal range), which is consistent with the findings of the primary and secondary efficacy endpoints, and the reported treatment effect of ocrelizumab. Two cases of treatment-emergent ADAs were treatment-induced. In OPERETTA 1 no ADAs were detected. The low frequency of treatment emergent or induced ADAs is in line with findings in adult OPERA 1 and 2 trials (about 1%). Neutralizing antibodies were not investigated in OPERETTA 2 due to the low frequency in the adult population (2/1311 patients) which is considered an acceptable uncertainty. The exploratory analyses of efficacy in OPERETTA 1 (open-label, no comparator) are supportive of the findings in OPERETTA 2. MRI-findings support reduced disease activity, and while keeping in mind the limitations of the study design, it is arguably rather unlikely that an ineffective treatment would have been able to nearly obliterate radiological signs of continued disease activity, in the long-term, in 22/23 patients. Nevertheless, no firm conclusions on the clinical efficacy of ocrelizumab in POMS should be drawn based on OPERETTA 1, because of the small sample size, the lack of blinding and lack of comparator. It is noted that long-term observation is available for the majority of the included patients, and that OPERETTA 1 includes a broader age (10-17) and weight range (2 patients &lt; 35 kg)

than OPERETTA 2.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The results from the adult pivotal studies, OPERA 1 and 2, confirmed the efficacy of ocrelizumab in RMS. Ocrelizumab is widely used for treatment of RMS (including in patients with high disease activity), and has shown long-term efficacy in adults, i.e. the OLE of OPERA 1 and 2. Considering the recognized pathophysiological and overall clinical similarities between POMS and AOMS, there is a strong biological rationale for use of B-cell depleting therapies, with known efficacy in AOMS, in patients with POMS. The fact that POMS is often associated with highly inflammatory and active MS strengthens the rationale for use of B cell depleting therapies and/or HE-DMTs in POMS. The findings in OPERETTA 2 reflect those seen in adults. This is confirmatory of the biological rationale for use of ocrelizumab in POMS, especially considering the fact that data are obtained in a controlled design. As noted previously in this AR residual uncertainties include long-term sustained efficacy in POMS and long-term progression of disability. This is discussed elsewhere.

The MAH provided 5 publications with real world data. The provided publications are overall supportive of the use of ocrelizumab in POMS, although it must be noted that several methodological concerns apply (trial design, sample size, lack of comparator). Further, only sparse information about long-term efficacy or treatment of patients aged 10-12 years old could be found in the published literature provided by the MAH, thus real world data is not able to reduce these uncertainties further.

## 2.4.4. Conclusions on the clinical efficacy

The main clinical trial, OPERETTA 2, was considered to be in compliance with the PIP and the study design was generally acceptable. The study met its primary endpoint of non-inferiority to fingolimod for ARR in paediatric onset RMS and, secondary endpoints (namely MRI) and subgroup analyses were supportive of this finding. Overall, efficacy has been shown in the paediatric patients equal to or above 10 years of age.

## 2.5. Clinical safety

## Introduction

Ocrelizumab is a recombinant humanized monoclonal antibody that selectively depletes cluster of differentiation (CD)20-expressing B cells.

Ocrelizumab IV is approved for the treatment of adult patients with RMS or PPMS.

This document describes the safety profile of ocrelizumab in pediatric patients with RRMS.

The safety of ocrelizumab in pediatric patients with RRMS, is based on data from two interventional studies study WN42086 (OPERETTA 2) and WA39085 (OPERETTA 1). The safety data derived from the OPERETTA 1 and OPERETTA 2 studies were not pooled: they were analysed separately and are presented individually due to several key distinctions between the study designs and patient populations.

## Patient exposure

Safety data for OPERETTA 2 are presented for all randomized patients who received at least one dose of study drug, with patients grouped according to actual treatment received. Data are provided up to the CCOD of the primary analysis (9 June 2025), conducted after the last patient randomized had completed at least 24 weeks since their first blinded ocrelizumab or ocrelizumab placebo infusion, as reported in the OPERETTA 2 primary CSR 1138543.

Safety data for OPERETTA 1 are presented for all patients who received at least one dose of the study drug, with patients grouped by their initial dose cohort. Data are provided up to the CCOD used for the update analysis (20 February 2025), when the last patient enrolled completed at least 96 weeks of treatment, as reported in the OPERETTA 1 update CSR.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## OPERETTA 2

Patients were considered to be exposed to the study drug if they have received at least one dose (complete or partial) of the study drug (n=93 OCR and n=92 FINGO).

The mean (SD) treatment duration was similar between the two arms: 80.2 weeks (35.5) for patients exposed to ocrelizumab and 76.7 weeks (37.1) for patients exposed to fingolimod.

## OPERETTA 1

All 23 patients enrolled in the study received at least one dose of ocrelizumab. As of the CCOD for the Update CSR (20 February 2025) The mean (SD) treatment duration was 188.5 (45.5) for the total group of patients exposed to ocrelizumab in OPERETTA 1 and the median number of doses received was 9 (range: 4-12).

- In Cohort 1, five of the six patients switched from the 300 mg dose to the 600 mg dose (one patient withdrew from the study without having reached the BW threshold for switching). Patients' exposure to the 300 mg dose ranged from 335 to 870 days (approximately 47.9 to 124.3 weeks). Following the switch, patients' exposure to the 600 mg dose ranged from 336 to 1092 days (approximately 48 to 156 weeks). Note that the cumulative dose in mg for patients in Cohort 1 reflects total exposure to the 300 mg and 600 mg combined. Therefore, the cumulative dose should be considered independently of the number of doses of ocrelizumab.
- For Cohort 2, the cumulative dose in mg is directly proportional to the number of doses, as all patients received the 600 mg ocrelizumab at each dosing visit.

## Common adverse event

## OPERETTA 2

The most frequent AEs by PT in the OCR group were: IRR (48.4% for ocrelizumab vs. 23.9% for fingolimod), upper respiratory tract infection (31.2% for ocrelizumab vs. 27.2% for fingolimod), headache (29.0% for ocrelizumab vs. 25.0% for fingolimod), nasopharyngitis (20.4% for ocrelizumab vs. 15.2% for fingolimod), influenza (14.0% for ocrelizumab vs. 5.4% for fingolimod), fatigue (9.7% for ocrelizumab vs. 7.6% for fingolimod), sinusitis (9.7% for ocrelizumab vs. 3.3% for fingolimod), gastroenteritis (8.6% for ocrelizumab vs. 7.6% for fingolimod), nausea (7.5% for ocrelizumab vs. 12.0% for fingolimod), diarrhoea (7.5% for ocrelizumab vs. 8.7% for fingolimod), urinary tract infection (7.5% for ocrelizumab vs. 6.5% for fingolimod), rhinitis (7.5% for ocrelizumab vs. 5.4% for fingolimod), dysmenorrhoea (7.5% for ocrelizumab vs. 4.3% for fingolimod), vomiting (7.5% for ocrelizumab vs. 3.3% for fingolimod), arthralgia (6.5% for ocrelizumab vs. 3.3% for fingolimod) and insomnia (6.5% for ocrelizumab vs. 0% for fingolimod).

## OPERETTA 1

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The most frequent AEs ( ≥ 50% of patients) by PT were: infusion-related reaction (82.6%), COVID-19 (60.9%), headache (60.9%), upper respiratory tract infection (56.5%).

## Cohort 1:

- The most frequent AEs ( &gt; 50%) by SOC were: Infections and infestations (100%); Injury, poisoning and procedural complications (83.3%); Nervous system disorders, Gastrointestinal disorders, General disorders and administration site conditions, Investigations (66.7%).
- The most frequent AEs (≥ 50% of patients) by preferred term (PT) were: IRR (83.3%); headache (66.7%); COVID-19, dizziness, fatigue, abdominal pain and nausea (50.0%).
- The most frequent events ( ≥ 5 events) were: headache (15), IRRs (8), dizziness and pyrexia (5).

## Cohort 2:

- The most frequent AEs (&gt;50%) by SOC were: Infections and infestations (100%); Injury, poisoning and procedural complications, Nervous system disorders (82.4%); Gastrointestinal disorders, General disorders and administration site conditions, Respiratory, thoracic and mediastinal disorders, Musculoskeletal and connective tissue disorders (58.8%).
- The most frequent AEs (≥50% of patients) by preferred term (PT) were: infusion-related reaction (82.4%); COVID-19 and upper respiratory tract infection (64.7%); headache (58.8%).
- The most frequent events (≥ 10 events) were: IRR (54), headache (34), upper respiratory tract infection (17), nasopharyngitis (13), COVID-19 (12).

## Adverse drug reactions

For labeling purposes, adverse drug reactions (ADRs) were identified based on data from the OPERETTA 2 DBP.

## Methodology

AEs were selected for further medical review by the Sponsor as follows:

- o AEs observed at a frequency of ≥ 5% and occurring with a higher frequency in the OCR arm than in the FINGO arm, by &gt; 2% magnitude of difference.
- o All AEs observed at a frequency of ≥ 5% in the OCR arm, and which are known ADRs for fingolimod, if they occurred at the same or higher frequency as in the FINGO arm, regardless of the magnitude of difference.
- o All serious AE (SAEs) observed in the OCR arm (to ensure that no medically significant event was missed).

Causality was assessed based on evidence of mechanistic plausibility, the current label, class effects, reversibility, temporality, coherence (between AEs and laboratory/vital signs results). Individual patient data were reviewed for existence of any risk factors that may explain the imbalance observed. The AEs, which were already listed in the ADRs table of the Sponsor's Core Data Sheet (CDS) v16.0 and met the threshold defined in the methodology were automatically considered as ADRs in the pediatric population.

There were no pediatric specific ADRs that were not previously identified in adults. Frequency categories for the pediatric population were consistent with those observed in the adult population (CDS version 16; OCREVUS® IV EU SmPC; OCREVUS® USPI).
