- Last/replicated abnormality of high alkaline phosphatase in 16.7% patients (1/6)
- Single abnormality of high chloride in 16.7% patients (1/6)
- Last/replicated abnormality of high phosphorus in 16.7% patients (1/6)
- Single abnormality of high sodium in 16.7% patients (1/6)
- Single abnormality of high triglycerides in 16.7% patients (1/6)

## Cohort 2

- Single abnormality of high serum glutamate pyruvate transaminase (SGPT)/ ALT and high serum glutamicoxaloacetic transaminase (SGOT)/ AST in 5.9% patients (1/17)
- Single abnormality of high creatine kinase in 17.6% patients (3/17)
- Single abnormality of high phosphorus in 11.8% patients (2/17)
- Single abnormality of triglycerides in 11.8% patients (2/17) and last/replicated abnormality in 5.9% patients (1/17)

## Adverse Events of Grade 3 or 4 Chemistry Parameters in OPERETTA 1

In Cohort 1, one patient experienced hypernatraemia (Grade 3). No action was taken with study drug. In Cohort 2, one patient experienced 14 AEs related to chemistry parameters (all had resolved by the CCOD with no action taken with study drug except where specified). Events of Grade 3 or 4 were:

- Alanine aminotransferase increased: one serious Grade 4 event assessed by the investigator as not related as it occurred prior to the first administration of study drug (which was interrupted) and resolving after 95 days; and one non-serious Grade 2 event assessed by the investigator as related to study drug, lasting 7 days
- Aspartate aminotransferase increased (two non-serious Grade 3 events, assessed by the investigator as related to study drug resolving after 7 days and one assessed by the investigator as not related to study drug, resolving after 95 days)
- Blood creatine phosphokinase increased (one serious Grade 4 event assessed by the investigator as related to study drug resolving after 21 days; study drug was interrupted

## Immunogenicity

## OPERETTA 2

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The prevalence of ADAs at baseline was 0.5% (1/183 patients). Of the 90 patients in the OCR arm with post-baseline assessments, 2 patients (2.2%) showed treatment emergent ADAs to ocrelizumab. The impact of treatment-emergent ADAs on efficacy and safety endpoints could not be determined, given the low incidence.

## OPERETTA 1

All 23 patients with at least one ADA assessment were included in the immunogenicity evaluation. At baseline and up to the CCOD, no treatment-emergent ADAs to ocrelizumab were detected in this study.

## Immunology and serology

Quantitative Immunoglobulin Levels

## OPERETTA 2

At baseline, the proportion of patients with IgA, IgG and IgM results &lt;LLN was 5.4%, 3.2% and 0%, respectively. At Week 46, the proportion of patients with IgA, IgG and IgM results &lt;LLN was 15.1% for IgA, 8.2% for IgG and 38.4% for IgM.The OCR treatment arm showed a decrease in IgM (38.4% of patients with IgM below the LLN at Week 46). Additionally, there was a slight decrease in IgG, and a largely stable IgA level with a mild increase in proportion of patients below LLN at later time points.

In OPERETTA 1, no clinically meaningful difference was observed for median immunoglobulin (IgA and IgG) concentrations from baseline to the CCOD in both cohorts. IgM levels decreased with similar pattern as seen in the pooled adult RMS population.

The FINGO treatment arm generally showed a decrease across all three immunoglobulin types (at Week 46 [when 66 patients had immunoglobulin assessments], the proportion of patients with levels below LLN were 6.1% for IgG, 15.2% for IgA, and 10.6% for IgM.

## OPERETTA 1

In both treatment cohorts, no clinically meaningful difference was observed for median immunoglobulin (IgA and IgG) concentrations from baseline to the CCOD. IgM levels decreased with similar pattern as seen in the pooled adult RMS population.

Overall, 5 patients (5/23; 21.7%) experienced AEs assessed by the investigator as related to decreases in levels of blood immunoglobulin as follows:

- Cohort 1: 2 patients (2/6;33.3%) experienced non-serious Grade 1 events of blood IgM decreased. One of these patients (1/6;16.7%) also a non-serious Grade 1 event of blood IgG decreased. These events were not associated with any infection. The events had not resolved at the time of CCOD.
- Cohort 2: 2 patients (2/17; 11.8%) experienced non-serious Grade 1 events of blood IgM decreased which had not resolved at the time of CCOD, and 1 patient (1/17; 5.9%) experienced a non-serious Grade 1 event of blood IgG decreased, resolving without treatment after 135 days. These events were not associated with any infection.

None of the events resulted in dose interruption or discontinuation from the study. All events were assessed by the investigator as related to study treatment.

## Antibody Titers to Common Antigens

In both studies, measurement of antibody titers to antigens from the mumps, rubella, varicella, and S. pneumoniae (pneumococcus) was performed. Antibody titers were positive for the majority of the patients at baseline and at subsequent timepoints post-study treatment, indicating preserved humoral immunity to these antigens over time.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Vital signs

## OPERETTA 2

In infusion visits, the median diastolic blood pressure in both treatment arms showed slight fluctuations within a narrow range. The infusions in both the OCR and FINGO arms were not associated with any major consistent shifts in systolic blood pressure.

During infusion visits, the pulse rates in the OCR arm were increased slightly following the start of infusion, but the median rates were still within normal range. The trend was not observed in noninfusion visits in the OCR arm. In the FINGO arm, the pulse rates showed minor fluctuations in a narrow range for both infusion and non-infusion visits.

Respiratory rate and temperature remained relatively stable in both treatment arms during infusion visits and non-infusion visits.

Two patients experienced hypotension, both of which resolved without treatment after 1 day:

- One patient in the OCR arm experienced a non-serious Grade 3 event. Ocrelizumab dose was not changed or delayed.
- One patient in the FINGO arm experienced a non-serious Grade 1 event. Fingolimod was not interrupted.

## OPERETTA 1

Both systolic and diastolic blood pressure showed a trend towards lower values over the course of the study, particularly in Cohort 2. Pulse rate, respiratory rate, and temperature remained relatively stable. Height and weight both increased as expected during the study period, with more pronounced height changes observed in Cohort 2 compared to Cohort 1.

When IRRs were accompanied by vital signs readings not within predefined thresholds, the vital signs results were queried for clinical significance: in most of the cases the investigators did not deem these vital signs results as clinically significant.

In Cohort 1, no clinically meaningful difference from baseline to the CCOD was observed for the median vital sign values for all parameters. Two patients presented abnormalities in their vital signs associated with adverse events (none of which were related to an IRR):

- One patient experienced two non-serious AEs of Grade 2 pyrexia.
- One patient experienced non-serious Grade 1 pyrexia on three occasions.

In Cohort 2, 2 patients presented abnormalities in their vital signs associated with adverse events (none of which were related to an IRR):

- One patient experienced a serious Grade 3 event of hypotension. The event was assessed by the investigator as not related to study drug and resolved after 3 days. Study drug was not changed.
- One patient experienced a non-serious Grade 2 event of hypertension.
- Seven patients experienced a total of 8 events of Grade 1 or Grade 2 pyrexia. None of the events were assessed by the investigator as related to study drug.

All events were resolved at the time of reporting.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Electrocardiography

## OPERETTA 2

## OCR

Two patients in the OCR arm had clinically significant ECG abnormalities in their post-baseline assessments at baseline (Week 1) at the end of the 6 hour blinded fingolimod observation period. Neither of these patients presented cardiac disorders as part of their medical history. These ECG abnormalities were concurrent with IRRs observed with the first dose:

- One patient in the OCR arm had an ECG assessment at Day 1 (at the end of the 6-hour fingolimod [placebo] observation period), which was clinically significant. This was accompanied by nonserious Grade 1 AEs of Electrocardiogram ST segment elevation and Atrioventricular block first degree on Day 1, resolving after 1 day without change to study drug. This was concurrent with a non-serious Grade 1 IRR (symptom: flushing). The ECG at Week 2 was abnormal but not clinically significant, and thereafter ECGs were normal for Weeks 24, 48, 72 and 96 (dosing visits).
- One patient had an ECG assessment at Day 1 (at the end of the 6-hour fingolimod [placebo] observation period), which was clinically significant. This was accompanied by non-serious AE of Grade 1 Atrioventricular block first degree on Day 1, which resolved after 1 day. Thereafter, ECG assessments were normal (except for one abnormal and not clinically significant assessment at Week 48 prior to dosing).

## FINGO

1 patient in the FINGO arm had clinically significant ECG abnormalities in their post-baseline assessments at baseline (Week 1) post infusion. The ECG assessment at the end of the 6 hour fingolimod observation period was normal. All ECGs at all timepoints thereafter were normal. This patient had an AE of serious Grade 2 Electrocardiogram QT prolonged; no other AEs on this day.

One patient had a serious Grade 1 AE of sinus bradycardia on Day 1, not associated with abnormal findings on their ECG assessments. The event resolved after 3 days.

Two other patients had non-serious Grade 1 cardiac-related AEs:

- One patient had AEs of Early repolarisation syndrome and Supraventricular extrasystoles on Day 1. These resolved after 1 day with no change to study drug.
- One patient had an AE of tachycardia. The event resolved after 1 day.

## OPERETTA 1

## Cohort 1:

- All patients had normal ECG readings at baseline, three of whom had no abnormal ECG readings post-baseline.
- One patient experienced a shift from normal to an abnormal but not clinically significant ECG reading.
- Two patients experienced a clinically significant ECG abnormality compared to a normal reading at baseline.
- o One patient had a non-serious Grade 1 event of left bundle branch block, as reported in the Primary CSR; this event was associated with non-clinically significant abnormal ECG readings. This patient later had clinically significant abnormal ECG readings pre- and post-

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

- dose which were not accompanied by adverse events (this was also prior to the primary CCOD).
- o One patient had a clinically significant abnormal ECG reading, which was associated with an AE of IRR (non-serious, Grade 2, with symptom of electrocardiogram ST segment abnormal).

## Cohort 2:

- At baseline, 13 patients had a normal ECG reading. During the study, 5 of these patients had no abnormal ECG readings; 6 patients showed at least one non-clinically significant post-baseline ECG reading, and 2 patients showed at least one clinically significant post-baseline ECG reading.
- o Two clinically significant abnormal ECG readings were recorded for one patient. Both readings were associated with the non-serious Grade 1 event of ECG QT prolonged, which had a duration of 143 days.
- o One patient had a clinically significant abnormal ECG reading, which was associated with an AE of IRR (non-serious, Grade 1) with the symptom of electrocardiogram QT prolonged.
- 4 patients had abnormal but not clinically significant ECG readings at baseline, and at least one reading post-baseline.

## Safety in special populations

## Intrinsic factors

In adults, safety data from patients with RMS pooled from two pivotal Phase III studies (WA21092 [Opera 1] and WA21093 [Opera 2]) indicated no appreciable differences in the AE profile of ocrelizumab as a function of sex, BW, race, cardiovascular disease, diabetes, or prior MS DMT. Therefore, no specific intrinsic factors analyses were conducted for pediatric patients, except for a subgroup analysis for OPERETTA 2 by baseline age ≥ 14 years old and &lt; 14 years old (in line with subgroup analysis by age at baseline as performed for the primary endpoint of annualized relapse rate).

No subgroup analyses were conducted for OPERETTA 1, due to the small sample size overall (n = 23) and the fact that the two cohorts were characterized separately (Cohort 1 n = 6 and Cohort 2 n = 17). In Cohort 1, all patients were &lt; 14 years old at baseline (range: 10-12 years); in Cohort 2, 2 patients were &lt; 14 and 15 patients were ≥ 14 years.

## Baseline Age ≥ 14 Years old and &lt; 14 Years Old

A subgroup analysis was performed to characterize the AE profile for patients aged 14 years and older, versus those aged under 14 years.

Considering the limitations in direct comparisons due to differences in subgroup sizes (79 patients aged ≥ 14 years and 14 patients aged &lt; 14 years in the OCR arm, respectively; 73 patients aged ≥ 14 years and 19 patients aged &lt; 14 years in the FINGO arm, respectively), no direct comparison was performed. Total PY at risk in the OCR arm was 116.22 years and 20.54 years in the older and younger age groups, respectively. Total PY at risk in the FINGO arm was 100.03 years and 30.08 years in the older and younger age groups, respectively.

Three patients were pre-pubertal: 1 patient was in the OCR arm and 2 patients were in the FINGO arm. Two patients (one in the OCR arm and one in the FINGO arm) did not experience any AEs; one patient in the FINGO arm experienced 19 AEs of variable nature. Of the 19 AEs observed, none were serious, and all were either Grade 1 or 2. All except one event of cough had resolved by the time of the CCOD, with durations ranging from 1 to 14 days. None of the events resulted in modification or interruption of the fingolimod dose (or placebo ocrelizumab dose).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Plots of immunoglobulin IgA, IgM, IgG levels (over time were provided for the older and younger age groups. The patterns observed in immunoglobulin levels across patients in the OCR arm of either age subgroup were similar.

## Extrinsic factors

In adults, safety data from patients with RMS pooled from two pivotal Phase III studies (Opera 1 and Opera 2) indicated no appreciable differences in the AE profile of ocrelizumab as a function of geographical region, or concomitant steroid use in patients with MS. Therefore, no specific analyses of extrinsic factors were conducted for pediatric patients.

## Safety related to drug-drug interactions and other interactions

No formal drug-drug interaction studies have been performed, as no drug-drug interactions are expected via the CYP and other metabolizing enzymes or transporters for a mAb like ocrelizumab.

## Discontinuation due to adverse events

## Adverse Events that led to Withdrawal of Treatment

## OPERETTA 2

No patients in the OCR arm experienced AEs that led to withdrawal of study treatment.

3.3% of patients (3/92) in the FINGO arm experienced a total of 4 events leading to withdrawal of study treatment (transaminase increased, alanine aminotransferase increased, mental disorder, toxicity to various agents). When adjusted for treatment exposure, there were 3.07AE/100PY (95% CI: 0.84, 7.87). For 2 patients, the events were serious.

The events of transaminase increased and alanine aminotransferase increased are known side effects typically associated with fingolimod. The events of mental disorder and toxicity to various agents (paracetamol intake) in the same patient are described in the patient narrative, and are not typically associated with fingolimod.

## OPERETTA 1

No patients in Cohort 1 experienced AEs leading to discontinuation of study treatment. In Cohort 2, 1 patient (5.9%) withdrew from study treatment during OOE due to a chronic viral infection of Grade 2. (When adjusted for treatment exposure, there was 1.20AE/100PY leading to discontinuation of treatment [95% CI: 0.03, 6.7]). The event was non-serious and not resolved at the time of reporting. The patient entered the SFU period after study treatment discontinuation, and started treatment with commercial ocrelizumab, however later died.

## Adverse Events that led to Dose Modification

## OPERETTA 2

No patients in the OCR arm experienced AEs that led to dose interruption (i.e., full dose not given) or delay of active ocrelizumab.

In the FINGO arm, 8.7% of patients experienced a total of 15 AEs that led to interruption of active fingolimod (i.e., interrupted for a short period of time). When adjusted for extent of exposure, there were 11.53AE/100PY leading to interruption of fingolimod (95% CI: 6.45, 19.01). AEs leading to interruption of fingolimod were primarily in the SOCs Infections and infestations and Blood and lymphatic system disorders, as may be expected by the ADR profile and mechanism of action.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The incidence of SAEs leading to interruption of fingolimod in the FINGO arm per 100PY were 1.54 (0.19, 5.55).

## OPERETTA 1

No patients in Cohort 1 experienced AEs that led to dose delay. 11.8% of patients in Cohort 2 (8.7% overall) experienced AEs that led to dose delay but were reported as dose interruptions, however the dose was not modified. Both patients received the full dose of 600 mg ocrelizumab after the AEs resolved.

- One patient experienced serious Grade 4 events of alanine aminotransferase increased and blood creatinine phosphokinase increased on separate occasions, resolving after 95 and 21 days, respectively.
- The second patient experienced 2 non-serious events of Grade 1 and Grade 2 neutrophil count decreased on separate occasions. Both events were assessed by the investigator as related to study drug, did not require treatment and had resolved within 18 and 22 days.

The rate of all AEs leading to infusion interruption, per 100PY was 4.8 (95% CI:1.3, 12.3) (total PY at risk: 83.1 years). The rate of serious AEs leading to infusion interruption was 2.4 (95% CI: 0.3, 8.7).

## Post marketing experience

Post-marketing data summarized in this section originates from the experience of adult patients with ocrelizumab 600 mg IV and 920 mg SC, and off-label use in pediatric patients. Data are taken from the latest Periodic Benefit-Risk Evaluation Report (PBRER) 1137774 (with data lock point 27 March 2025) which was submitted to health authorities per local regulatory requirements.

Since the International Birth Date (IBD, 28 March 2017) until 31 March 2025, an estimated cumulative total of 408,744 patients have received ocrelizumab from marketing experience. This is equivalent to an estimated 1,274,195 patient-years of exposure since IBD. Since the Developmental International Birth Date (23 September 2003), an estimated total of 13,383 patients have received ocrelizumab in clinical trial setting.

Overall, the case reports received and reviewed during the post marketing period (up to 27 March 2025) were consistent with the known safety profile of ocrelizumab seen in the clinical trial setting. The overall safety evaluation of the post-marketing data are consistent with the safety profile observed in the clinical trial data, with longer treatment duration, as well as with exposure of broader and more heterogeneous population.

Ocrelizumab has a well-established safety profile and favorable risk-benefit in the approved indications, as reflected in the approved labels. No additional risk-minimization activities are in place for ocrelizumab.

## Off-Label Use in Pediatric Patients

In the most recent PBRER, 536 cases (1286 events) were reported from non-interventional study (NIS)/ non-interventional program (NIP) sources (n=339; including 29 cases from literature NIS/NIP), spontaneous sources (n=171; including 7 cases from literature spontaneous) and clinical trials (n=26) (PBRER 1137774). Age distribution for these patients is presented in Table 19.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

Table 19: Age Range for the Pediatric Population

| Age (in Years)   |   Number of Cases |
|------------------|-------------------|
| < 1 to < 14 a    |                56 |
| 14 to < 18       |               334 |
| Unknown          |               146 |
| Total            |               536 |

Overall, the indication reported in these cases included RRMS (n = 200 cases), MS (n = 154 cases), RMS (n = 78 cases), PPMS (n = 8 cases), lupus nephritis (n = 5 cases), MS relapse (n = 3), demyelination, vasculitis (n = 2 cases, each), and one case each of secondary progressive MS, chronic inflammatory demyelinating polyradiculoneuropathy, disruptive mood dysregulation disorder and optic neuritis. In the remaining cases (n = 80), the indication was not reported.

The most frequently reported PTs in these cases were off label use (n = 303), no adverse event (n = 232), infusion related reaction (n = 66), intentional product use issue (n = 41), fatigue (n = 37), headache (n = 22), nausea (n = 16), and infection (n = 5). The remaining PTs accounted for less than 1% of the reported events. The frequently reported PTs were consistent between the patients aged &lt; 14 years and 14 to &lt;18 years and no new pattern was identified in the reported events.

No safety signals and no new safety concerns related to the safety of ocrelizumab in the pediatric population have been identified to date.

## 2.5.1. Discussion on clinical safety

The safety of ocrelizumab is well known for the treatment of adult patients with RMS and early PPMS. This assessment will focus on the clinically significant differences in safety for paediatric patients compared to the known safety profile of ocrelizumab in adult patients.

## Safety data

The safety assessment of ocrelizumab in this submission is based on data from OPERETTA 2 and OPERETTA 1 studies. The safety data of the two studies was not pooled, due to the different study designs. This is considered acceptable.

In OPERETTA 2, the pivotal phase III study, 187 patients were randomized in a 1:1 ratio to receive either ocrelizumab (OCR arm) or fingolimod (FINGO arm). The median age of patients was 15 years (range: 11-17 years). A total of 33 patients were &lt;14 years of age. A total of 3 pre-pubertal patients (defined as Tanner stage &lt;2) were randomized, 1 patient in the OCR arm and 2 patients in the FINGO arm.

93 patients received at least one dose of ocrelizumab 600 mg and 92 patients received at least one dose of 0.5 mg fingolimod. The mean (SD) treatment duration was 80.2 weeks (35.5) for patients exposed to ocrelizumab and 76.7 weeks (37.1) for patients exposed to fingolimod. In the ocrelizumab arm, the median number of doses received was 4 (range: 1-7). 66 patients (71%) received ocrelizumab for at least one year and 28 patients (30.1%) received ocrelizumab for more than 2 years in OPERETTA 2.

According to the latest PIP (18 August 2025) at least 145 and 71 randomised patients was planned to be followed up for at least 48 and 96 weeks, respectively. At the data cut-off, 137 patients were followed for 48 weeks and 64 patients were followed for 96 weeks in the total group in OPERETTA 2.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The patient counts differences between the numbers in the PIP and the presented exposure, results from study withdrawals during the DBP. Overall, the extend of exposure for OPERETTA 2 is in concordance with the PIP, with a study period of at least 24 weeks, a safety follow up for at least 48 weeks counting from the date of the last infusion of ocrelizumab, at least 171 randomised patients, at least 30 patients less than 14 years of age and a stratification analysis carried out for pre-pubertal patients.

The MAH provided additional safety-data with the cut-off date 9 September 2025. Overall, no new safety concerns were identified in the additional safety data.

In OPERETTA 1, the phase II dose-finding study, 6 patients with a BW &lt;40 kg (Cohort 1) initially received at least one dose of ocrelizumab 300 mg and 17 patients with a BW of ≥ 40 kg (Cohort 2) received at least one dose of ocrelizumab 600 mg. In Cohort 1, 5 of the 6 patients switched from the 300 mg dose to the 600 mg dose and one patient withdrew from the study without having reached the BW threshold for switching. The mean (SD) treatment duration was 188.5 (45.5) for the total group of patients exposed to ocrelizumab in OPERETTA 1 and the median number of doses received was 9 (range: 4-12). 23 patients (100%) received ocrelizumab for at least one year and 21 patients (91.3%) received ocrelizumab for more than 2 years in OPERETTA 1. The extend of exposure for OPERETTA 1 is in concordance with the PIP, with at least 6 patients per dose level who completed the 24 weeks study, an optional extension of at least 72-weeks and a safety follow up period for at least 48 weeks counting from the date of the last infusion of ocrelizumab.

It is agreed that long-term safety is missing information for the paediatric population. The MAH has updated the missing information \"Long-term safety in pediatric population\" in the RMP to include the relation to the potential long-term safety concerns addressed in the PIP for the paediatric population; Infection including PML, malignancies and autoimmune disease as requested.

## Adverse events

In OPERETTA 2, at least one AE was reported in 83 (98.2%) patients in the OCR group and 72 (78.3%) patients in the FINGO group. The difference can be attributed to difference between the two groups in the frequency of IRRs (48.4% vs. 23.9%) and infections (73.1% vs. 58.7%). This difference is further assessed in the sections with selected AE below. SAE occurred in 6 (6.5%) patients in the OCR group and 8 (8.7%) patients in the FINGO group. Grade 3-4 AEs were reported for 10 (10.8%) patients in the OCR group and 17 (18.5%) patients in the FINGO group. AEs leading to withdrawal from treatment only occurred in the FINGO group (3 patients (3.3%)). No Adverse event with fatal outcome were reported at the CCOD.

Overall, the frequencies of the different categories of AEs (e.g. AEs, SAEs, Grade 3-4 AEs etc.) in study OPERETTA 2 were similar to the observed frequencies in the initial marketing authorisation application in adults, except for patients with at least one AE. Again, this difference can be attributed to a higher frequency of IRRs in the paediatric patients compared to the adult RMS population (40.1% in adult RMS patients vs. 48.4% in paediatric RRMS patients in OPERETTA 2).

In OPERETTA 1, at least one AE was reported in all patients (100%) both in Cohort 1 and Cohort 2. Serious adverse events occurred in 2 (33.3%) in Cohort 1 and 4 (23.5%) in Cohort 2. Grade 3-4 AEs were reported for 3 (50.0%) patients in and 7 (41.1%) patients in Cohort 2. AEs leading to withdrawal from treatment occurred in 1 (5.9%) patient in Cohort 1 and 1 (4.3%) patient in Cohort 2. No AE with fatal outcome were reported.

Overall, the safety profile of ocrelizumab 300 mg administered to patients in Cohort 1 appeared to be similar with the safety profile of ocrelizumab 600 mg administered to patients in Cohort 2. However, the small numbers of patients in the two cohorts in OPERETTA 1 preclude any firm conclusions.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Common adverse events

Overall, the most common AEs were consistent with the known safety profile of ocrelizumab in adults. However, as previous stated IRRs were reported more frequent in the paediatric population compared to the adult population in the initial marketing authorisation application. Furthermore, dysmenorrhoea (7.5% vs. 4.3%), rhinitis (7.5% vs. 5.4%), vomiting (7.5% vs. 3.3%), arthralgia (6.5% vs. 3.3%) and insomnia (6.5% vs. 0%) were reported more frequent (&gt; 2% difference) in the OCR group compared to the FINGO group. Most AEs in the OCR arm for dysmenorrhea, rhinitis, vomiting, arthralgia and insomnia were assessed as unrelated to ocrelizumab and alternative causes were identified. Moreover, the rates in the study were generally lower than in general paediatric and adolescent population. Therefore, it is agreed that dysmenorrhea, rhinitis, vomiting, arthralgia and insomnia are not considered an ADR for ocrelizumab.

## Adverse Events by Intensity

In OPERETTA 2, adverse events were mostly mild to moderate in severity (78.5% for ocrelizumab vs. 59.8% for fingolimod). Grade 3 events were reported less frequently in the OCR arm than the FINGO arm (10.8% vs. 15.2% of patients). IRRs were reported as Grade 3 only in the OCR arm (3.2% vs. 0% of patients). In the SOC Infections and infestations more patients had grade 3-4 events in the OCR arm compared to the FINGO arm (3.2% vs. 2.2% of patients). There were no Grade 4 AEs in the OCR arm and no Grade 5 AEs in both arms. IRRs and infections are discussed further below.

In OPERETTA 1, adverse events were mostly mild to moderate in severity (516/546). Grade 3 events (27/546) were reported in 10 patients and Grade 4 (3/546) events were reported in 2 patients. One patient had a serious Grade 4 neutropenia (assessed by the investigator as not related to study drug) and another patient had two Grade 4 events, both of which were serious: alanine aminotransferase increased and blood creatinine phosphokinase increased. These cases are further assessed in the section Laboratory findings. There were no notable differences in intensity of AEs between the two Cohorts.

## Deaths

As of the respective CCODs, no deaths were reported in OPERETTA 2 or OPERETTA 1. One death was reported in OPERETTA 1 after the CCOD and the event is considered unrelated to ocrelizumab. This is agreed.

## Serious Adverse Events

SAE were reported for 6.5% of patients in the OCR arm and 8.7% of patients in the FINGO arm in OPERETTA 2. The most frequent SAEs by SOC in the OCR arm was infections and infestations (3.2% in the Ocrevus arm vs. 3.3% in the fingolimod arm). SAE of Pneumonia was reported in 2 patients (2.2%) in the OCR arm (both assessed as related to ocrelizumab by the investigator) and 1 patient (1.1%) in the FINGO arm (assessed as unrelated to fingolimod by the investigator). All other events occurred in 1 patient each. One patient in the OCR arm (assessed as related to ocrelizumab by the investigator) and 0 in the FINGO arm experienced an SAE of IRR. In the OCR arm, all SAEs had resolved by the time of the CCOD.

In OPERETTA 1, 26.1% of patients experienced at least one SAE. All events occurred in 1 patient each. One patient experienced 2 SAEs of Grade 4 intensity ALT increased (assessed as unrelated by the investigator) and blood creatinine phosphokinase increased (assessed as related to ocrelizumab by the investigator). These events are further assessed in the section Laboratory findings. All SAEs except for one event of anal abscess were resolved as of the CCOD (anal abscess resolving).

Overall, the SAEs reported does not raise any new safety concerns.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Adverse Events of Special Interest

None were reported in both studies.

## Selected Adverse Events

In OPERETTA 2, 48.4% of patients had at least one IRR in the OCR arm compared to 23.9% in the FINGO arm (of note, the infusion in the FINGO arm was placebo). The proportion of patients with IRRs decreased over time for both treatment arms. In the OCR arm, most of the IRRs was mild or moderate in intensity (45.2% of patients) and most of the events resolved in 1-2 days. Three patients experienced Grade 3 IRRs and there were 1 serious event occurring at the first dose, assessed as related to ocrelizumab by the investigator. The patient had symptoms of itching, coughing, flushing and throat irritation. The event resolved after one day and did not result in change or delay to ocrelizumab dosing. There were no IRRs of Grade 4 or 5. In the OCR arm, the most common symptoms of IRRs were headache, throat irritation, pruritus, rash, erythema and nausea.

In OPERETTA 1, 82.6% of patients had at least one IRR. The incidence of IRRs was similar between the two cohorts (83.3% in Cohort 1 and 82.4% in Cohort 2). The highest incidence of IRRs was reported at Dose 1 (56.5%). In the subsequent doses there was variability in IRR frequency with no consistent pattern (0%-31.8%). Most of the events resolved in 1-2 days. All of the IRRs was mild or moderate in intensity, except for 3 Grade 3 events in Cohort 2. There were no IRRs of Grade 4 or 5. All events were non-serious. Common symptoms were headache, throat irritation, rash, fatigue and myalgia.

Overall, the rate of IRRs was higher in OPERETTA 1 than in OPERETTA 2. The study population of the OPERETTA 1 study was smaller than that of the OPERETTA 2 population but patients in OPERETTA 1 were treated for approximately twice as long as patients in OPERETTA 2. It is noted that the risk of IRRs decreased over time in OPERETTA 2, whereas this was not observed in OPERETTA 1. This is most likely explained by recurrent IRRs (&gt;5 events) in 6 patients (3 of these patients had potential risk factors for IRR; medical history of seasonal allergy). These 6 patients can have impacted the overall results in OPERETTA 1, given its small sample size.

Compared to the known safety profile of ocrelizumab in adult patients, IRRs were reported more frequent in the paediatric population (40.1% in adult RMS patients vs. 48.4% in paediatric RRMS patients in OPERETTA 2). The MAH suggested that e.g. heightened immune reactivity, treatment naivety, PK/PD differences and increased disease activity in the paediatric patients could explain the differences between adults and children. The MAH has updated the SmPC section 4.8 with a subsection under the ADR table describing the IRRs in paediatric patients. The information is considered relevant for the prescriber and is acceptable. The MAH discussed if the higher frequency of IRRs in the paediatrics should be reflected in the ADR table in the SmPC. Since the frequency for both adults and paediatric patient fall into the category \"very common\" and that the frequency in adult and paediatric patient is outlined in the subsections under \"Descriptions of selected adverse reactions\" in the SmPC section 4.8, it is agreed, that it is not necessary to outline the difference in the ADR table.

There were no events of PT anaphylaxis or PT hypersensitivity in OPERETTA 2. In OPERETTA 1, 17.4% of patients (4/17) experienced PT hypersensitivity. All of the events were grade 1-2 and none of the events were serious. None of these events were considered by Sponsor medical review to be true hypersensitivity reactions. No cases of hypersensitivity or anaphylactic reaction related to study treatment was identified after the sponsors medical review of the MedDRA Anaphylactic reactions SMQ (Broad) and the Hypersensitivity SMQ (Broad) in Both OPERETTA 2 and OPERETTA 1.

In OPERETTA 2, the proportion of patients with at least one infection was higher in the OCR arm (73.1%, with 204 events) compared to the FINGO arm (58.7%, with 152 events). This difference was mostly driven by differences in incidence of upper respiratory tract infection (31.2% vs. 27.2%), nasopharyngitis (20.4% vs. 15.2%) influenza (14.0% vs. 5.4%), sinusitis (9.7% vs. 3.3%) and rhinitis (7.5% vs. 5.4%). These infections are listed as \"very common\" ADRs in the SmPC except for rhinitis. Even though there is a difference for rhinitis, rhinitis was not included in the ADR table in the SmPC because alternative causes of rhinitis were identified in 5 of the 7 patients in the OCR arm. The incidence of COVID-19 was similar in both treatment arms (4.3% of patients in each treatment arm).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The proportion of patients with at least one serious infection was balanced between the two treatment arms (3.2% vs. 3.3%). The most frequent serious infection by PT was pneumonia (2.2% in the OCR arm and 1.1% in the FINGO arm). A case-level review found no association between serious infections and patients' immunoglobulin levels &lt; LLN in either treatment arm.

In OPERETTA 1, all patients experienced at least one AE within the SOC of infections and infestations. In Cohort 1, there was one SAE of appendicitis reported in 1 patient. All other reported PTs were nonserious, with a most extreme intensity of Grade 1 or 2. In Cohort 2, a total of 6 serious infections of Grade 3 were reported in 1 patient. The reported events included four SAEs which were assessed by the investigator as related to study drug. The majority of infections (91/98) reported in Cohort 2 were non-serious with a most extreme intensity of Grade 1 or 2. No events of opportunistic infection were reported.

Overall, the infections reported in the two studies are in line with the known safety profile of Ocrevus in adults. The MAH has updated the SmPC section 4.8 with a subsection describing the infections in OPERETTA 2. This is endorsed.

In the PIP it is stated that infections including PML are potential long-term safety issue in relation to paediatric use for consideration in the RMP/Pharmacovigilance activities. The Safety specification in the RMP has been updated accordingly.

In OPERETTA 2, one patient had a non-serious Grade 2 AE of neutrophil count decreased on Day 1 of the study in the OCR arm. The patient did not receive treatment for this event, which resolved after 8 days without change to study treatment. The patient had neutropenia at the study start. In OPERETTA 1, 2 patients had one event of neutrophil count decreased.

Neutropenia is a known ADR for ocrelizumab in adults. The MAH has updated the SmPC section 4.8 with information on decreased neutrophil counts in paediatric patients. Here, the MAH states that no decreased neutrophil counts were observed in ocrelizumab treated patients. The information is acceptable, since only one patient in OPERETTA 2 had an event of neutrophil count decreased and this patient had neutropenia before study treatment.

In the OCR arm in OPERETTA 2, one patient had non-serious Grade 1 AE of psoriasis. The patient had medical history of psoriasis before study treatment. Another patient experienced a non-serious Grade 1 AE of butterfly rash. In the FINGO arm, one patient experienced a non-serious event of Grade 1 psoriasis. The patient had medical history of hand dermatitis and fungal infection before study treatment. In OPERETTA 1, one patient experienced a non-serious event of Grade 1 psoriasis in Cohort 2. The patient had medical history of autoimmune thyroiditis.

Due to the limited number of autoimmune diseases and potential risk factors, no conclusions can be drawn on the potential risk of autoimmune disease in the paediatric population with ocrelizumab treatment. In the PIP it is stated that autoimmune diseases are a potential long-term safety issue in relation to paediatric use for consideration in the Risk Management Plan/Pharmacovigilance activities. The Safety specification in the RMP has been updated accordingly.

No events of malignancies were identified in OPERETTA 2 or OPERETTA 1. In the PIP it is stated that malignancies are a potential long-term safety issue in relation to paediatric use for consideration in the Risk Management Plan/Pharmacovigilance activities. The Safety specification in the RMP has been updated accordingly.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Laboratory findings

Overall, no new findings in haematology parameters were identified for ocrelizumab in paediatric

patients. In OPERETTA 2, there were no patients with severe liver injury as defined by Hy's law in either treatment arm. However, any marked abnormality for high ALT was observed in 20% of patients in the OCR arm and 35.2% of patients in the FINGO arm and any marked abnormality for high AST was observed in 19.5% of patients in the OCR arm and 23.9% in the FINGO arm. During the preparation of the D90SUR, a programming discrepancy was identified in the primary analysis for OPERETTA 2. Instead of using the Sponsor's standard template, a different approach was used in error, defining marked abnormalities as values not only outside the Roche standard reference range but also outside the 10th and 90th percentiles derived from the observed data. This led to an overreporting in the OPERETTA 2 CSR. For the D90SUR analysis, the Sponsor's standard template was used, defining marked abnormalities as values outside the Sponsor's standard reference range and exhibiting a clinically significant change from baseline. The MAH provided the corrected data with the Sponsor's standard template. Any marked abnormality for high ALT was observed in 1.1% of patients in the OCR arm and 3.3% of patients in the FINGO arm and any marked abnormality for high AST was observed in 3.2% of patients in the OCR arm and 0% in the FINGO arm. Adverse Events related to AST increased were reported in 3.2% in the OCR arm and 2.2% in the FINGO arm. ALT increased was reported in 2.2% in the OCR arm and 2.2% in the FINGO arm. In the OCR arm, one patient had a non-serious Grade 3 ALT increased, assessed by the Investigator as related to study treatment; one patient had non-serious Grade 3 AST increased, assessed by the Investigator as unrelated to study treatment; and one patient had non-serious Grade 3 hepatic enzyme increased, assessed by the Investigator as related to study treatment. With the new corrected data from OPERETTA 2, the frequency of high ALT and AST decreased substantially. However, the levels are similar to fingolimod and hepatic enzyme increased (increased ALT, GGT, AST) is a very common known ADR of fingolimod. Moreover, two Grade 3 hepatic enzyme increased was assessed as related to ocrelizumab. A causal relationship for elevated ALT and AST cannot be excluded. As a result of EMAVR0000313041 procedure, drug-induced liver injury (DILI) is included in the SmPC as a new ADR, but transient LFT elevations will not, as they are not considered causally associated with ocrelizumab treatment. The Grade 3 events were not serious and were single, transient occurrences that resolved spontaneously within 43 to 84 days, despite continued ocrelizumab exposure. In contrast, fingolimod treatment resulted in additional safety concerns associated with the Grade 3 LFT elevations, including a case of serious transaminase increase and a recurrent adverse event of Grade 3 ALT increased. The differences in the observed hepatic enzyme increases between ocrelizumab and fingolimod explains their designation as ADRs in the fingolimod SmPC, but do not warrant the inclusion as a separate ADR in the proposed ocrelizumab SmPC. This is acknowledged. cohorts. In Cohort 2, there was one serious Grad 4 event of ALT increased (this event was assessed by

In OPERETTA 1, there were no patients with severe liver injury as defined by Hy's law in either of the the investigator as not related as it occurred prior to the first administration of study drug), two nonserious Grade 3 events of AST increased (assessed by the investigator as related to study drug) and one serious Grade 4 event of Blood creatine phosphokinase increased (assessed by the investigator as related to study drug).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

In OPERETTA 2, 2 patients (2.2%) showed treatment-emergent ADAs to ocrelizumab. In OPERETTA 1, there were no treatment-emergent ADAs to ocrelizumab. This information has adequately been implemented to the SmPC section 5.1. The information provided in the SmPC section 5.1, that the impact of treatment-emergent ADAs on safety and efficacy cannot be assessed given the low incidence of ADA associated with ocrelizumab is considered adequate for the paediatric population.

In OPERETTA 2, a decrease in IgM and IgG was seen in the OCR arm. IgA was more stable, but with a mild increase in proportion of patients below LLN. In OPERETTA 1, no clinically meaningful difference was observed for median immunoglobulin (IgA and IgG) concentrations from baseline to the CCOD in both cohorts. IgM levels decreased with similar pattern as seen in the pooled adult RMS population.

None of the events resulted in dose interruption or discontinuation from the study. All events were assessed by the investigator as related to study treatment.

Overall, the pattern of immunoglobulin decreases seen in the paediatric patients was similar to the immunoglobulin decrease observed in adult patients in the initial marketing authorisation application. Blood immunoglobulin M decreased and Blood immunoglobulin G decreased are already known ADRs in the SmPC. The MAH added an adequate subsection in the SmPC section 4.8 describing the decrease in immunoglobulins in the paediatric patients.

## Vital signs and Electrocardiography

No clinically relevant changes from baseline were seen for vital sign parameters over time and no patterns of clinically significant changes in vital signs were identified for ocrelizumab in both OPERETTA 2 and OPERETTA 1.

Overall, abnormalities in ECG findings in the two studies were rare and there were no serious adverse events accompanied the abnormal ECG findings.

## Safety in special populations

The MAH states that, safety data from patients with RMS, pooled from two pivotal Phase III studies (WA21092 and WA21093), in adults indicated no appreciable differences in the AE profile of ocrelizumab as a function of sex, BW, race, cardiovascular disease, diabetes, prior MS DMT geographical region or concomitant steroid use in patients with MS and therefore, no specific intrinsic factors or extrinsic factors analyses were conducted for paediatric patients, except for a subgroup analysis for OPERETTA 2 by baseline age ≥14 years old and &lt;14 years old. It is not completely agreed there were no differences in the AE profile for the mentioned subgroups in adults; according to the EPAR from the initial MAA, differences in the frequencies of AEs and SAEs were observed in BW, diabetes, concomitant steroid use and region. On request, the MAH provided overall summary tables for the intrinsic factors; sex, baseline body mass index and the extrinsic factor; region. Subgroup analyses by medical condition and concomitant steroid use were not conducted due to low number of patients within the specified subgroups. This is acknowledged.

The overall safety profile of female and male patients was similar. However, higher rates of AEs were seen in patients with BMI ≥95 th percentile and U.S. patients. That MAH states that this was driven by three patients from the same U.S. site reporting 35, 37, and 43 AEs, which reflects the local reporting practice. Two of the three patients also belonged to the baseline BMI ≥95th percentile group, which drove the observed difference in this subgroup analysis.

The overall safety profile of patients ≥14 years old and &lt;14 years old were similar. Plots of immunoglobulin levels over time showed no meaningful differences between the two age groups.

## Drug-drug interactions

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

No formal drug-drug interaction studies have been performed. This is acceptable, since no drug-drug interactions are expected via the CYP and other metabolizing enzymes or transporters for a monoclonal antibody like ocrelizumab.

The safety of immunisation with live or live-attenuated vaccines, following ocrelizumab therapy has not been studied. Due to its mode of action, it is not recommended to use other immunosuppressive therapies concomitantly with ocrelizumab except corticosteroids for symptomatic treatment of relapses. This is reflected in the SmPC section 4.5.

## Discontinuations due to adverse events

No patients in the OCR arm in OPERETTA 2 experienced AEs that led to withdrawal, dose interruption or delay of study treatment.

In in Cohort 1 in OPERETTA 1, no patients experienced AEs leading to discontinuation of study treatment or dose delay. In Cohort 2, 1 patient (5.9%) withdrew from study treatment during OOE due to a chronic viral infection of Grade 2 and 2 patients (11.8%) had AEs that led to dose delay. The first patient experienced serious Grade 4 events of ALT increased and blood creatinine phosphokinase increased on separate occasions, resolving after 95 and 21 days, respectively. The second patient experienced 2 non-serious events of Grade 1 and Grade 2 neutrophil count decreased on separate occasions. Both events were assessed by the investigator as related to study drug, did not require treatment and resolved within 18 and 22 days.

## Post marketing experience

Overall, the post-marketing safety data are in line with the known safety profile of ocrelizumab. No new safety concerns are raised.

## 2.5.2. Conclusions on clinical safety

Overall, available safety data from the two paediatric studies OPERETTA 2 and OPERETTA 1 in patients with RRMS is consistent with the known safety profile for ocrelizumab.

## 2.5.3. PSUR cycle

The requirements for submission of periodic safety update reports for this medicinal product are set out in the list of Union reference dates (EURD list) provided for under Article 107c(7) of Directive 2001/83/EC and any subsequent updates published on the European medicines web-portal.

## 2.6. Risk management plan

The MAH submitted to submit an updated RMP version with this application.

The CHMP received the following PRAC Advice on the submitted Risk Management Plan:

The PRAC considered that the risk management plan version 16.2 is acceptable.

The CHMP endorsed this advice without changes.

## 2.7. Update of the Product information

As a consequence of this new indication, sections 2, 3, 4.1, 4.2, 4.4, 4.5, 4.6, 4.8, 5.1, 5.2 and 6.6 of the SmPC have been updated. The Package Leaflet has been updated accordingly.

Changes were also made to the PI to bring it in line with the current Agency/QRD template, SmPC guideline and other relevant guideline(s) [e.g. Excipients guideline, storage conditions, Braille, etc…], which were reviewed by QRD and accepted by the CHMP.

In addition, the list of local representatives in the PL has been revised to amend contact details for the representative(s).

## 2.7.1. User consultation

The results of the user consultation with target patient groups on the package leaflet submitted by the MAH show that the package leaflet meets the criteria for readability as set out in the Guideline on the readability of the label and package leaflet of medicinal products for human use.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3. Benefit-Risk Balance

## 3.1. Therapeutic Context

## 3.1.1. Disease or condition

RMS is typically considered a diagnose of younger adults with a typical onset about 29-30 years of age. Published guidelines and consensus statements agree that a single diagnostic framework should be applied to AOMS and POMS. Further, the current diagnostic consensus guidelines (Montalban et al., 2025) state that RMS in adults and children share similar pathobiological mechanisms and MRI features. Paediatric patients cannot per se be considered to have a milder disease, although they may recover more quickly from relapses, because it is well recognized that the paediatric population often experience high disease activity, higher relapse rates, and may reach disability milestones at a younger age than adults due to earlier onset (Montalban et al., 2025, PIP for ocrelizumab).

## 3.1.2. Available therapies and unmet medical need

In the EU teriflunomide is indicated for paediatric RMS without restriction in the age group 10-17 years. Dimethyl fumarate has a paediatric indication for RMS in children aged 13 years or older. Fingolimod is indicated for treatment of paediatric RMS in children aged 10-17 years old, but is restricted to those with highly active and/or treatment refractory RMS.

Considering the highly active disease experienced by paediatric patients, and the well documented risk of disability later in life, there is a clear unmet need for highly-effective DMTs that can be used in the paediatric population. Fingolimod can be considered a HE-DMT although it has a different safety profile and mechanism of action, including risk of AV block and cardiological adverse effects, macular oedema which occurs in 0.5% of patients, and severe disease rebound after treatment discontinuation.

## 3.1.3. Main clinical studies

The pivotal study OPERETTA 2 was a phase III multicenter, randomized, double-blind, double-dummy, parallel-group, non-inferiority study to evaluate safety and efficacy of ocrelizumab in comparison with fingolimod in children and adolescents with relapsing-remitting multiple sclerosis. The double-blind period was flexible with at least 24 weeks of duration for the last patient randomised. There was an optional open-label extension and a safety follow-up period of at least 144 weeks and 48 weeks, respectively. The primary endpoint was annual relapse rate. 93 and 94 patients were randomised to ocrelizumab and fingolimod, respectively.

A smaller phase II dose-finding study, OPERETTA 1, was also conducted, testing 300 and 600 mg in 2 cohorts. 23 patients (6 patients in Cohort 1 and 17 patients in Cohort 2) were enrolled in the study, all of whom received at least 1 dose of ocrelizumab. All 23 patients completed the 24-week dose exploration period.

The doses in paediatric patients are weight dependent; those weighing less than 35 kg will receive half the adult dose, those weighing more than 35 kg will receive the adult dose.

## 3.2. Favourable effects

The study met its pre-defined primary endpoint of non-inferiority compared to fingolimod. The point estimate favoured ocrelizumab, with a 48% lower risk of relapse (adjusted rate ratio of 0.517) and an adjusted ARR of 0.070 (0.034, 0.144) in ocrelizumab versus 0.135 (0.075, 0.243) in the fingolimod arm. The upper bound of the 95% CI was well within the pre-defined NI-margin of 3. Subgroup analyses were generally confirmatory of the non-inferiority of ocrelizumab (presence of T1 Gd enhancing lesions, recent corticosteroid use ≤ 30 days prior to randomization, prior DMT use, disease activity).

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

A pre-defined superiority analyses for the primary endpoint was non-confirmatory and ocrelizumab was not superior to fingolimod (ARR rate 0.517, [95% CI: 0.193, 1.329]).

Generally, the number of protocol defined relapses was low in both arms. In the ocrelizumab arm 9 patients had 10 relapses during the trial, 6/10 occurred before 24 weeks with 4 occurring before 12 weeks. In the fingolimod arm 13 patients had 16 relapses, of which 6/16 (37.5%) occurred in the first 24 weeks of the trial and 5/16 (31%) occurred before week 12.

The annualized rate of new/enlarging hyperintense T2 lesions after treatment was 3.827 (95% CI 2.931, 4.996) per year in the ocrelizumab arm versus 7.275 (95% CI 5.606, 9.442) per year in the fingolimod arm. The pre-defined superiority analysis of the same endpoint showed that ocrelizumab is superior to fingolimod in terms of radiological disease control (N/E T2 lesions rate ratio 0.522 [95% CI 0.359, 0.760]).

The rate of T1 Gd enhancing lesions at 12 weeks was 0.054 (95% CI 0.017, 0.125) in the ocrelizumab arm versus 0.375 (95% CI 0.258, 0.527) in the fingolimod arm. The adjusted rate ratio showed superiority of ocrelizumab (0.128 [95% CI: 0.034, 0.414]) compared to fingolimod. Both superiority analyses relating to brain MRI were placed above the breaking point in the statistical testing hierarchy.

## 3.3. Uncertainties and limitations about favourable effects

Not unexpectedly, due to the rarity of MS in children (2-3% with POMS are &lt;12 years old, (Benallegue et al. 2024)), only few patients aged 10-11 years were included in the OPERETTA studies. One 11year-old in OPERETTA 2, OCR arm and five 10-11-year-olds in OPERETTA 1. In the 10-12-year-old age group (OPERETTA 1+2) 3/16 patients were pre-pubertal as defined by Tanner stage &lt; 2 (3/16 unknown). Thus, there is some clinical evidence for use in patients aged 10-12 and/or pre-pubertal patients although somewhat limited.

The flexible duration of the study was 24 weeks, and a short trial duration of 24 weeks would normally be questioned in the context of DMTs for MS. However, the average patient was in the study for much longer (about 80 weeks) reducing uncertainties about the period of relapse observation and assay sensitivity of the active comparator. Analyses of ARR at week 48 and 96 ocrelizumab were reassuring, with no sign of loss of efficacy, and can be considered supportive of the sustained efficacy of ocrelizumab in POMS.

Considering the high risk of accrual of severe, permanent disability conferred by the natural history of both paediatric and adult RMS, the endpoint of long-term disability could be considered the most important endpoint. Inherent to clinical trials in RMS this endpoint is difficult to investigate due to the long duration from diagnosis to disability. This is especially true in paediatric patients who rarely accrue severe and/or permanent disability in childhood and/or adolescence. In adult trials 24 week confirmed disability progression is often used, although this endpoint is less favoured in paediatric trials due to low rates of disability progression. In OPERETTA 2 is was analysed as an exploratory endpoint. 12 patients (n=12.9%) in the ocrelizumab arm and n=13 patients (14.1%) had a 24-week CDP event during the trial. This is higher than expected, however, the MAH was able to provide further analyses which suggest that this was due to different definitions of 12- and 24-week CDP across paediatric trials. Furthermore, the MAH provided 5 months of OLE data from OPERETTA 2 (ocrelizumab arm only) which showed sustained efficacy. By way of the trial design long-term sustained efficacy and long-term effect on disability outcomes is a residual uncertainty which cannot be completely solved. To some extent, this is also the case for adult trials. Considering the totality of the evidence this uncertainty is considered acceptable and does not preclude a positive benefit risk.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## 3.4. Unfavourable effects

In OPERETTA 2, the most frequent AEs by PT in the OCR group were IRR (48.4% for ocrelizumab vs. 23.9% for fingolimod), upper respiratory tract infection (31.2% for ocrelizumab vs. 27.2% for fingolimod), headache (29.0% for ocrelizumab vs. 25.0% for fingolimod), nasopharyngitis (20.4% for

ocrelizumab vs. 15.2% for fingolimod) and influenza (14.0% for ocrelizumab vs. 5.4% for fingolimod). Overall, the most common AEs were consistent with the known safety profile of ocrelizumab in adults. However, compared to the known safety profile of ocrelizumab in adult patients, IRR were reported more frequent in the paediatric population (40.1% in adult RMS patients vs. 48.4% in paediatric RRMS patients in OPERETTA 2). The MAH suggested that e.g. heightened immune reactivity, treatment naivety, PK/PD differences and increased disease activity in the paediatric patients could explain the differences between adults and children. In OPERETTA 2, the proportion of patients with at least one infection was higher in the OCR arm (73.1%) compared to the FINGO arm (58.7%). The proportion of patients with at least one serious infection was balanced between the two treatment arms (3.2% vs. 3.3%). The most frequent serious infection by PT was pneumonia (2.2% in the OCR arm and 1.1% in the FINGO arm). In OPERETTA 1, all patients experienced at least one AE within the SOC of infections and infestations. No events of opportunistic infection were reported. Overall, the infections reported in the two studies are in line with the known safety profile of Ocrevus in adults. In OPERETTA 2, any marked abnormality for high ALT was observed in 1.1% of patients in the OCR arm and 3.3% of patients in the FINGO arm and any marked abnormality for high AST was observed in 3.2% of patients in the OCR arm and 0% in the FINGO arm. AEs related to AST increased were reported in 3.2% in the OCR arm and 2.2% in the FINGO arm. ALT increased was reported in 2.2% in the OCR arm and 2.2% in the FINGO arm. In the OCR arm, one patient had a non-serious Grade 3 ALT increased, one patient had non-serious Grade 3 AST increased and one patient had non-serious Grade 3 hepatic enzyme increased. associated with ocrelizumab treatment. The Grade 3 events were not serious and were single, transient

The levels for elevated ALT and AST are similar to fingolimod and hepatic enzyme increased (increased ALT, GGT, and AST) is a very common known ADR of fingolimod. Moreover, two Grade 3 hepatic enzyme increased was assessed as related to ocrelizumab. A causal relationship for cannot be excluded. As a result of EMAVR0000313041 procedure, drug-induced liver injury (DILI) is included in the SmPC as a new ADR, but transient LFT elevations will not, as they are not considered causally occurrences that resolved spontaneously within 43 to 84 days, despite continued ocrelizumab exposure. In contrast, fingolimod treatment resulted in additional safety concerns associated with the Grade 3 LFT elevations, including a case of serious transaminase increase and a recurrent adverse event of Grade 3 ALT increased. The differences in the observed hepatic enzyme increases between ocrelizumab and fingolimod explains their designation as ADRs in the fingolimod SmPC, but do not warrant the inclusion as a separate ADR in the proposed ocrelizumab SmPC. This is acknowledged.

SAE were reported for 6.5% of patients in the OCR arm and 8.7% of patients in the FINGO arm in OPERETTA 2. The most frequent SAEs by SOC in the OCR arm was infections and infestations (3.2% in the Ocrevus arm vs. 3.3% in the fingolimod arm). SAE of Pneumonia was reported in 2 patients (2.2%) in the OCR arm (both assessed as related to ocrelizumab by the investigator) and 1 patient (1.1%) in the FINGO arm (assessed as unrelated to fingolimod by the investigator). All other events occurred in 1 patient each. One patient in the OCR arm (assessed as related to ocrelizumab by the investigator) and 0 in the FINGO arm experienced an SAE of IRR.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

As of the respective CCODs, no deaths were reported in OPERETTA 2 or OPERETTA 1.

## 3.5. Uncertainties and limitations about unfavourable effects

For the paediatric patients, long-term safety in relation to infection including PML, malignancies and autoimmune disease are missing information and a potential risk that need to be handled in the RMP.

## 3.7. Benefit-risk assessment and discussion

## 3.7.1. Importance of favourable and unfavourable effects

The study met its pre-defined primary endpoint of clinical non-inferiority compared to fingolimod based on protocol-defined relapses (ARR). Subgroup analyses were generally confirmatory of the noninferiority of ocrelizumab. A pre-defined superiority analyses for the ARR was non-confirmatory and ocrelizumab was not superior to fingolimod (ARR rate 0.517, [95% CI: 0.193, 1.329]). In terms of radiological disease activity, ocrelizumab was superior compared to fingolimod with respect to T2 lesions during the DBP (rate ratio 0.522 [95% CI 0.359, 0.760]) and T1 lesions at week 12 (rate ratio 0.128 [95% CI: 0.034, 0.414]) compared to fingolimod. The magnitude of this difference is considered clinically relevant and the secondary radiological endpoints support the conclusion of non-inferiority to fingolimod. The efficacy of ocrelizumab appear to be similar to that observed in adults with RMS.

The safety profile of ocrelizumab is consistent with its mechanism of action (mAb that selectively targets CD20 expressing B cells) with reported cases of infusion-related reactions, infections, neutropenia and decreases in immunoglobulins. There are serious identified risks (infusion-related reactions and infections) and potential risks (malignancies and PML) with ocrelizumab therapy which is considered to be manageable with the current labelling and monitoring. For the paediatric patients, long-term safety in relation to infection including PML, malignancies and autoimmune disease are missing information and a potential risk that need to be handled in the RMP.

With the caution needed due to the limitations of the safety database, particularly in the long-term, it appears that the safety profile of ocrelizumab in paediatric patients with RRMS is manageable and overall consistent with the known safety profile in adults.

## 3.7.2. Balance of benefits and risks

The primary endpoint of non-inferiority to fingolimod for ARR in paediatric onset RMS was met and overall supported by secondary endpoints (namely MRI) and subgroup analyses.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

The safety profile of ocrelizumab in paediatric patients with RRMS was overall consistent with its mechanism of action and the known safety profile in adult patients. The identified risks are considered manageable.

## 3.7.3. Additional considerations on the benefit-risk balance

None

## 3.8. Conclusions

The overall B/R of Ocrevus (300 mg concentrate for solution for infusion [intravenous use]) is positive for paediatric patients aged 10 years and older with relapsing-remitting multiple sclerosis (RRMS).

## 4. Recommendations

## Outcome

Based on the review of the submitted data, the CHMP considers the following variation acceptable and therefore recommends by consensus the variation to the terms of the Marketing Authorisation, concerning the following change:

| Variation accepted   | Variation accepted                                                          | Type    | Annexes affected   |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------|--------------------|
| C.I.6.a              | Addition of a new therapeutic indication or modification of an approved one | Type II | I, IIIB            |

Extension of indication to include treatment of paediatric patients aged 10 years and older with relapsing remitting multiple sclerosis (RRMS) for OCREVUS, based on primary analysis results from the pivotal phase III study (WN42086/OPERETTA 2) and primary and updated results from a supportive phase II study (WA39085/OPERETTA 1). OPERETTA 1 is an open-label, parallel-group, dose-finding Phase II study to determine the dosing regimen of ocrelizumab to be further investigated in OPERETTA 2, and OPERETTA 2 is a Phase III, randomized, double-blind, double-dummy, parallel-group, multicenter, non-inferiority study to evaluate the efficacy and safety of intravenous ocrelizumab in comparison with fingolimod. As a consequence, sections 2, 3, 4.1, 4.2, 4.4, 4.5, 4.6, 4.8, 5.1, 5.2 and 6.6 of the SmPC are updated. The Package Leaflet is updated in accordance. Version 16.2 of the RMP is endorsed. In addition, the Marketing authorisation holder (MAH) took the opportunity to introduce updates to other sections of the SmPC and PL as per previous procedures linguistic review comments (sodium, pH and osmolality), updates to comply with the Excipient Guideline (polysorbates), changes to the list of local representatives in the Package Leaflet, as well as editorial and clarification changes to the product information (PI).

The variation leads to amendments to the annex(es) I, and IIIB and to the Risk Management Plan (RMP).

## Amendments to the marketing authorisation

In view of the data submitted with the variation, amendments to Annex(es) I, and IIIB and to the Risk Management Plan are recommended.

<div style=\"page-break-after: always\"></div>

## Conditions or restrictions with regard to the safe and effective use of the medicinal product

## · Risk management plan (RMP)

The MAH shall perform the required pharmacovigilance activities and interventions detailed in the agreed RMP presented in Module 1.8.2 of the Marketing Authorisation and any agreed subsequent updates of the RMP.

In addition, an updated RMP should be submitted:

At the request of the European Medicines Agency;

Whenever the risk management system is modified, especially as the result of new information being received that may lead to a significant change to the benefit/risk profile or as the result of an important (pharmacovigilance or risk minimisation) milestone being reached.

## Paediatric data

In this procedure, the CHMP reviewed the available data from the studies conducted in accordance with the agreed PIP EMA/PE/0000265579, namely study 1 (OPERETTA 1 WA39085) and Study 2 (OPERETTA 2 WN42086). The results of these studies are reflected in sections 4.1, 4.2, 4.8, 5.1, and 5.2 of Summary of Product Characteristics (SmPC) for Ocrevus. 300mg, concentrate for solution for infusion and, as appropriate, the Package Leaflet. Further, these results were pivotal in supporting the new indication of paediatric patients aged 10 years and older with relapsing-remitting multiple sclerosis (RRMS).
